# DECISION B1 — RESEARCH-QUESTION IDENTIFICATION

**Status:** RATIFIED
**Upstream Authority:**
1. PROJECT MINORE — FOUNDING DEFINITION v0.1
2. DECISION D1 — CONCEPT UNDERSTANDING: RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION LIFECYCLE
3. DECISION A1 — RESEARCH INPUT ADMISSIBILITY AND PROVENANCE
4. DECISION A2 — CLAIM EXTRACTION PROCEDURE
5. A2-C1 and A2-C2 (BINDING)
6. DECISION A3 — CLAIM CLASSIFICATION
7. A3-C1 (BINDING)
8. DECISION A4 — CONCEPT ASSOCIATION
9. DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE
10. DECISION A6 — RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION CONSTRUCTION
11. DECISION A7 — INTERPRETATION EVOLUTION TRIGGERS
12. A7-C1 and A7-C2 (BINDING)

---

## 1. Exact Semantic Distinctions

| Term | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| **Research question** | A substantively grounded question about the research domain that the researcher identifies as worth investigating. May arise from documented conditions in the current research state or from researcher-generated inquiry introducing new research directions within the domain. |
| **Source claim** | What a source said (A1–A2). Immutable and attributable (D1). Research questions may arise from claims but are not themselves claims. |
| **Researcher interpretation** | The current authoritative understanding of a concept (A6). Research questions may identify what the interpretation does not adequately address or may investigate the interpretation itself, but identifying a question does not modify the interpretation. |
| **Unresolved contradiction** | Claims that cannot both be true simultaneously (A5), preserved without forced reconciliation. May be an origin for research questions but does not automatically become one. |
| **Research gap** | An aspect of the research domain where the current interpretation or available claim landscape provides insufficient, uncertain, incomplete, or absent understanding. |
| **Arbitrary curiosity** | Speculation lacking articulated substantive domain-grounded reasoning or connection to research objectives. |
| **Research-state-responsive inquiry** | Questions arising from documented conditions in the current research state (contradictions, gaps, tensions, limitations, anomalies). |
| **Researcher-generated grounded inquiry** | Questions introducing new research directions within the domain, grounded in substantive domain reasoning and research objectives, even without prior documentation in the current research state. |
| **Hypothesis** | A testable proposition formulated in response to a research question (later stages; not B1). Research questions precede hypotheses. |
| **Research-question origin** | The condition that gives rise to identifying a research question — may be a documented research-state condition or a researcher-generated new research direction. |
| **Question resolution** | Achieving sufficient understanding to consider a research question answered or settled. Resolution may occur through interpretation construction, empirical evidence, or further source discovery. |

---

## 2. Minimum Options

### Option A — Automatic Generation from Research-State Conditions
Every unresolved contradiction, gap, uncertainty, tension, variation, or anomaly automatically becomes a research question.

**Problem:** Creates unsustainable noise; treats all uncertainties as equally worthy of investigation; violates minimum-decision principle; removes researcher judgment; prevents prioritization or focus.

### Option B — Pure Researcher Designation without Grounding
Only the researcher's explicit designation creates a research question, without requiring substantive grounding in the research domain or objectives.

**Problem:** Arbitrary; permits baseless speculation; no accountability to research domain or objectives; undermines methodological rigor.

### Option C — Researcher-Identified Questions with Substantive Grounding
A research question is identified when:
- The researcher judges it worth investigating
- It is substantively grounded in either (a) documented conditions in the current research state, or (b) articulated domain-grounded reasoning introducing a new research direction
- It pertains to the research domain (A2-C1)

**Test against upstream:** Preserves researcher authority (D1) while requiring substantive grounding; does not restrict questions to documented deficiencies; maintains connection to research objectives; respects domain boundaries (A2-C1).

**Verdict:** Option C is the minimum viable and founding-consistent standard.

---

## 3. Research-Question Identification Procedure

The following procedure governs how the researcher identifies research questions.

**Step 1 — Identify potential origins**

Possible origins include:

**From documented research-state conditions:**
- Unresolved genuine contradictions (A5)
- Acknowledged tensions or variations in the available claim landscape
- Explicit gaps or limitations acknowledged in the current interpretation (A6)
- Uncertain or provisional aspects of the current interpretation
- Empirical anomalies or unexpected results from prior testing
- Downstream departures (A6 Rule 12) revealing interpretive ambiguities or limitations

**From researcher-generated inquiry:**
- New theoretical possibilities grounded in the research domain
- Novel comparisons or extensions within the domain
- Unexplored market phenomena within domain scope
- Questions arising from trading experience, external knowledge, or research objectives
- Deliberate expansion or refinement of research focus within domain boundaries

**Step 2 — Assess substantive grounding**

The potential question must satisfy:

**Domain relevance:** Does it pertain to trading knowledge, market behavior, price mechanics, trading methods, or concepts relevant to trading-model development (A2-C1)?

**Substantive grounding:** Either:
- (a) It addresses something not adequately answered by the current interpretation and available claim landscape, OR
- (b) It introduces a new research direction grounded in articulated domain reasoning and research objectives

A question is not adequately answered if:
- The current interpretation does not address it
- The interpretation explicitly acknowledges uncertainty, gaps, or limitations on the topic
- The interpretation provides a conceptual answer but empirical validation is absent and warranted
- The question investigates, evaluates, or extends the interpretation itself (rather than merely asking what the interpretation states)

**Articulated reasoning:** If the question arises from researcher-generated inquiry (b), the researcher must be able to articulate substantive domain-grounded reasoning for why it is worth investigating, even if that reasoning is not yet documented in source claims.

**Step 3 — Researcher identification**

The researcher formally identifies the question as worth investigating. This is a judgment call constrained by substantive grounding but not mechanically determined.

The researcher may:
- Identify the question and pursue it
- Acknowledge a potential question exists but defer or decline investigation
- Refine, narrow, or broaden a question before pursuing it

**Step 4 — Preserve the question without forcing resolution**

Identifying a research question does not require:
- Immediate answering
- Hypothesis formation
- Test design
- Empirical operationalization
- Interpretation modification

Research questions may remain open indefinitely.

---

## 4. Binding Methodology Rules

1. **A research question is substantively grounded in the research domain and research objectives.** It may arise from documented conditions in the current research state (contradictions, gaps, limitations, anomalies) or from researcher-generated inquiry that introduces new research directions within the domain, provided the researcher can articulate substantive domain-grounded reasoning.

2. **Not every contradiction, gap, uncertainty, tension, variation, anomaly, or curiosity automatically becomes a research question.** The researcher identifies which are worth investigating based on substantive grounding and judgment.

3. **Research-question identification is distinct from answering the question.** Identifying a question does not require knowing the answer, having a hypothesis, having a test design, or having empirical results. B1 concerns identification; later stages address answering.

4. **Research questions may arise from multiple origins, including researcher-generated new directions.** Valid origins include: documented research-state conditions (unresolved contradictions, tensions, gaps, limitations, uncertainties, empirical anomalies, downstream-departure findings) and researcher-generated grounded inquiry (new theoretical possibilities, novel comparisons, unexplored phenomena, deliberate research-focus expansion).

5. **A research question need not be immediately testable or empirically operationalizable at B1.** Empirical testability is addressed in later stages. Some research questions may be conceptual, definitional, or structural rather than empirical.

6. **Identifying a research question does not modify the current authoritative interpretation (A6).** The interpretation remains authoritative until explicitly revised. The question identifies what requires further investigation without changing what the researcher currently believes.

7. **Identifying a research question does not automatically trigger interpretation reconsideration under A7.** Research-question identification and A7 reconsideration triggers are distinct processes with different conditions. An A7 trigger (e.g., new contradictory claim, changed A5 status) may independently generate research questions, but identifying a research question does not itself create an A7 trigger.

8. **Research questions may remain open, be refined, narrowed, broadened, combined, split, or retired without being answered.** No mandatory resolution timeline or closure requirement exists.

9. **The researcher has authority to identify research questions.** This authority is constrained by substantive grounding in the research domain and research objectives, but within those constraints the researcher exercises judgment about what is worth investigating.

10. **Research questions must pertain to the research domain.** Per A2-C1, the domain is trading knowledge, market behavior, price mechanics, trading methods, or concepts relevant to trading-model development. Questions outside this domain are not Project Minore research questions.

11. **Questions asking what the current interpretation states are not research questions — the interpretation itself answers them.** However, questions investigating, evaluating, empirically testing, comparing, or challenging the current interpretation are valid research questions, provided the researcher has substantive grounds for the inquiry. The existence of an interpretation does not preclude research questions about whether the interpretation is empirically supported, complete, or optimal.

12. **Arbitrary curiosity is distinguished from legitimate inquiry by articulated substantive domain-grounded reasoning.** The discriminator is not whether the question already appears in the available claim landscape, but whether the researcher can articulate substantive domain-grounded reasoning connecting the question to research objectives.

---

## 5. Adversarial Tests

**Test 1 — Unresolved genuine contradiction as origin**

Current state: Two claims about Optimal Trade Entry level (50% vs. 61.8%) classified by A5 as Genuine Contradiction (unresolved). Current interpretation acknowledges the contradiction but provisionally adopts a position.

Potential research question: *"What is the empirically optimal retracement level for Optimal Trade Entry?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes (trading method)
- Grounding: Genuine unresolved contradiction in foundational claims
- Substantive basis: Yes
- Researcher judgment: If the researcher identifies this as worth investigating → Valid research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Unresolved contradiction provides documented research-state origin; researcher authority determines whether to pursue

---

**Test 2 — Researcher-generated exploratory inquiry vs. arbitrary curiosity**

Potential question: *"Do Fair Value Gaps exhibit different fill rates across different calendar periods (e.g., day of week, time of month)?"*

Sub-case A — Arbitrary curiosity:
- Researcher has no articulated reason beyond idle wondering
- No connection to research objectives or domain-grounded reasoning
- Classification: Arbitrary curiosity (invalid)

Sub-case B — Legitimate exploratory inquiry:
- Researcher articulates substantive reasoning: "External market research shows calendar effects in liquidity; I want to investigate whether this affects FVG behavior. This is not currently addressed in the claim landscape or interpretation, but it's a domain-relevant extension worth exploring."
- Domain-relevant: Yes (market behavior)
- Grounding: Articulated domain-grounded reasoning introducing new research direction
- Classification: Valid research question if researcher identifies it as worth pursuing

**Framework handling:** ✓ The discriminator is articulated substantive domain-grounded reasoning, not prior presence in claim landscape

---

**Test 3 — Gap in current interpretation**

Current interpretation: Defines Fair Value Gap structure but is silent on timeframe applicability (e.g., does FVG identification work the same on 1-minute vs. daily charts?).

Potential research question: *"On which timeframes are Fair Value Gaps reliably identifiable and behaviorally consistent?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Grounding: Explicit gap in current interpretation (timeframe scope unaddressed)
- Substantive basis: Yes (addresses documented limitation)
- If researcher identifies as worth investigating → Valid research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Gap in interpretation provides documented research-state origin

---

**Test 4 — Downstream departure revealing interpretive limitation**

Researcher attempts to operationalize Order Block identification for testing. Discovery: the interpretation is ambiguous about whether overlapping consolidations count as one block or multiple blocks.

Potential research question: *"How should overlapping consolidation structures be classified in Order Block identification?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Grounding: Interpretive ambiguity revealed by operationalization attempt (downstream departure)
- Substantive basis: Yes
- Valid research question origin

**Framework handling:** ✓ Downstream departure revealing limitation is valid documented research-state origin per Rule 4

---

**Test 5 — Minor variation not worth investigating**

Three sources describe slight timing variations (8:30 vs. 8:32 vs. 8:35) for the same session event within the same general concept.

Researcher assessment: The variation exists but is not material; the concept is adequately understood as "around 8:30."

- Potential question exists: "What is the exact optimal timing?"
- Researcher judgment: Not worth investigating; settled by "approximate timing" understanding
- No research question formally identified

**Framework handling:** ✓ Not every variation becomes a question; researcher authority applies

---

**Test 6 — Tension without clear investigative direction**

Two claims create interpretive tension about Market Maker intent (intentional vs. side-effect). Current interpretation brackets the contradiction.

Potential research question: *"Is Market Maker stop-loss hunting intentional or a side effect of natural order flow?"*

- Grounding: Yes (tension in available claim landscape)
- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Researcher may or may not identify this as worth investigating depending on whether intent affects trading-model construction
- If identified → Valid research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Researcher authority determines whether to pursue

---

**Test 7 — Empirical anomaly**

Prior backtest produced results contradicting a prediction embedded in the current interpretation (e.g., interpretation predicted FVGs fill 80%+ of the time; test showed 45%).

Potential research question: *"Under what conditions do Fair Value Gaps fill, and why did the test contradict the interpretation's prediction?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Grounding: Empirical anomaly
- Substantive basis: Yes (contradiction between interpretation and evidence)
- Valid research question origin

**Framework handling:** ✓ Empirical anomaly is valid documented research-state origin per Rule 4

---

**Test 8 — Question about what interpretation states vs. investigating the interpretation**

Sub-case A — Question about interpretation content:
*"What does our current interpretation define a Fair Value Gap as?"*

- Already answered: Yes, by current authoritative interpretation (provides complete definition)
- This is not a research question; refer directly to the interpretation document

Sub-case B — Empirical investigation of interpretation:
*"Does market data support our FVG definition, or do empirically-identified imbalances behave differently than our structural definition predicts?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Grounding: The interpretation exists (conceptual answer) but empirical validation is absent and warranted
- This is a valid research question per Rule 11 — existence of interpretation does not preclude empirical investigation

**Framework handling:** ✓ Rule 11 distinguishes conceptual questions (settled) from empirical/evaluative questions (valid)

---

**Test 9 — Question outside research domain**

Question: *"How do I code a Python backtest engine?"*

- Not about trading knowledge, market behavior, price mechanics, or trading methods
- Outside research domain per A2-C1 (technical implementation, not trading knowledge)
- Not a valid Project Minore research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Rule 10 enforces domain boundary

---

**Test 10 — Researcher-generated theoretical extension**

Current research state: Interpretations address Order Blocks and Fair Value Gaps separately.

Researcher-generated question: *"Do Order Blocks and Fair Value Gaps interact in predictable ways when they occur in proximity?"*

- Not currently addressed in claim landscape or interpretations
- No documented gap or contradiction exists about this interaction
- Researcher articulates reasoning: "Both are institutional-liquidity mechanisms; understanding their interaction could reveal higher-order patterns. This is a theoretically motivated extension worth exploring."
- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Grounding: Articulated domain reasoning introducing new research direction
- Valid research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Researcher-generated new direction is valid per Rule 1 and Rule 4

---

## 6. Ratified Decision

**Option C — Researcher-Identified Questions with Substantive Grounding**

B1 establishes that research questions are:
- Substantively grounded in the research domain and research objectives
- May arise from documented conditions in the current research state (contradictions, gaps, tensions, limitations, anomalies, empirical findings, downstream-departure discoveries) OR from researcher-generated grounded inquiry introducing new research directions
- Pertinent to the research domain (A2-C1 boundary)
- Identified by the researcher as worth investigating (researcher authority with substantive constraint)
- Distinct from answering, hypothesis formation, testing, or interpretation modification

Not every contradiction, gap, uncertainty, or curiosity becomes a research question. The researcher identifies which are worth pursuing based on substantive grounding and judgment.

The discriminator for arbitrary curiosity vs. legitimate inquiry is the researcher's ability to articulate substantive domain-grounded reasoning, not prior existence in the claim landscape.

---

According to the ratified dependency structure and Founding Definition mission pipeline:

- **M2 — Hypothesis Formation and Operationalization** is the next stage after B1. Research questions provide the input for hypothesis formation. M2 determines what makes a research question testable and how to formulate falsifiable hypotheses.

- Later stages (M3 — Experimental Rigor and Evidence Assessment, empirical testing, evidence evaluation) can now reference identified research questions as the origin of empirical work.

Point 1 Finding — Research Questions Must Arise from Current Research State
Exact tension:

B1 Step 2 requires: "Research-state connection: Does it address something not adequately answered by the current interpretation and available claim landscape?"

B1 Rule 1 requires: "A research question is substantively grounded in the current research state."

The task constraints explicitly stated: "Do not assume that a research question must originate only from a defect in the current interpretation."

B1 appears to require every research question to be grounded in an existing documented gap, contradiction, limitation, or deficiency in the current research state.

Upstream authority test:

The Founding Definition's Core Problem Q6 asks: "What does market data actually support or contradict?" This implies research questions about market behavior that may not yet be addressed by any existing interpretation or claim landscape.

Core Capability 6: "Identifying unanswered research questions." This does not restrict identification to gaps in the current interpretation — it may include genuinely new questions.

Necessity test:

Can a researcher legitimately ask: "Do liquidity patterns differ meaningfully across different market regimes?" even if:

No current interpretation addresses market regimes
No source claim mentions regimes
No contradiction, gap, or defect exists in the current research state regarding regimes
This could be:

A theoretically motivated inquiry
A novel research direction
An extension of the research domain
Grounded in the researcher's trading experience or external knowledge
Is this arbitrary speculation, or legitimate research-domain inquiry?

The distinction B1 needs is:

Arbitrary curiosity: baseless speculation with no substantive connection to the research domain or research objectives
Research-state-responsive inquiry: questions arising from documented gaps, contradictions, limitations in the current research state
Researcher-generated grounded inquiry: questions introducing new research directions, grounded in the research domain and research objectives, even without prior documentation in the current research state
B1 currently conflates the second and third categories under "grounded in current research state."

Yes. B1's current formulation makes "current research-state deficiency" a necessary condition for valid research questions. This is overly restrictive and contradicts the task constraint. It prevents the researcher from introducing genuinely new research directions.

Minimum correction required:

Revise Step 2 and Rule 1:

"Research-state connection: Does it address something not adequately answered by the current interpretation and available claim landscape, OR does it introduce a genuinely new research direction grounded in the research domain and research objectives?"

"A research question is substantively grounded in the research domain and research objectives. It may arise from documented conditions in the current research state (contradictions, gaps, limitations) or from researcher-generated inquiry that introduces new research directions within the domain."

Add a clarifying rule distinguishing arbitrary speculation from legitimate new-direction inquiry based on domain relevance and explicit researcher reasoning, not prior documentation.

Already settled conceptually: "What does our current interpretation define FVG as?" → Not a research question (just refer to the interpretation)

This is a complex task requiring me to produce Decision B1 — Research-Question Identification. Let me work through this systematically.

First, let me understand what B1 must accomplish:

Determine what constitutes a legitimate research question in Project Minore
Distinguish research questions from source claims, interpretations, contradictions, hypotheses, etc.
Establish conditions for identifying research questions
Preserve epistemic boundaries between identifying questions and answering them
Key constraints to respect:

Don't define hypothesis formation (that's B2/M2 territory)
Don't require empirical testability at this stage
Don't treat every contradiction/gap as automatically generating a research question
Don't modify the current authoritative interpretation
Don't conflate with A7 triggers
Keep question identification distinct from answering
Let me think through the semantic distinctions:

Research question vs. source claim: A source claim is what a source said; a research question is what the researcher wants to investigate about the domain
Research question vs. interpretation: An interpretation is the researcher's current understanding; a research question identifies what needs further investigation
Research question vs. contradiction: A contradiction is a conflict between claims; a research question may arise from that contradiction
Research question vs. hypothesis: A hypothesis is a testable proposition; a research question is broader and may not yet be testable
Research question vs. curiosity: Not everything the researcher wonders about is a formal research question
What are the minimum conditions for identifying a research question?

Looking at the Founding Definition Core Problem questions 1-8, these suggest the research process must answer specific questions. Some of these questions will generate research questions.

B1 should establish:

A research question must be substantively relevant to the research domain (A2-C1 boundary)
It must address something not yet adequately answered by the current interpretation or available claim landscape
It must have some substantive basis (not pure speculation)
It need not be immediately testable
Sources of research questions:

Unresolved contradictions (A5)
Tensions/variations in claim landscape
Gaps or limitations acknowledged in current interpretation
Downstream departures that reveal issues
Empirical anomalies
Researcher-generated inquiry grounded in the research domain
Not every contradiction automatically becomes a research question—the researcher must identify it as worth investigating. A research question is substantively grounded in the domain, arising from gaps, uncertainties, contradictions, or limitations in the current research state, and the researcher must judge it as meriting investigation.

The key distinctions matter: source claims are immutable and attributable; researcher interpretations represent current authoritative understanding; unresolved contradictions are claims that cannot both be true, preserved without forced resolution; research gaps occur where current understanding is insufficient or incomplete; hypotheses are testable propositions that come later; mere curiosity lacks substantive grounding in the domain or research state. The origin of a research question lies in whatever research-state condition prompted the researcher to identify it as worth pursuing.

Not all contradictions and gaps warrant investigation equally—treating every uncertainty as a potential research question creates unnecessary noise.

The researcher's explicit designation alone cannot ground a question without reference to the research state, yet substantive grounding requires the researcher's judgment about what matters. The viable approach combines both: a research question emerges when a genuine gap, uncertainty, contradiction, or limitation exists in the current research state and the researcher determines it merits investigation within the domain.

To identify research questions, I start by locating their origins in the research state itself—unresolved contradictions, acknowledged tensions, gaps in current interpretation, provisional or uncertain aspects, empirical anomalies, or downstream problems that reveal upstream issues.

Next, I assess whether the question actually pertains to the domain and addresses something the current interpretation leaves unanswered. Then the researcher formally identifies it as worth pursuing.

The key binding rules: substantive grounding in the research state is necessary but not sufficient—the researcher's judgment determines which gaps become actual questions. Identifying a question differs from answering it; no hypothesis or test design is required at this stage. Questions can originate from contradictions, tensions, gaps, anomalies, or other research-state conditions.

A question doesn't need to be immediately testable, doesn't alter the current authoritative interpretation, and doesn't automatically trigger reconsideration of that interpretation. Questions can remain open, be refined, or abandoned without resolution. The researcher holds authority here, though that authority is bounded by substantive grounding in what's already known.

Testing this: there's a genuine unresolved contradiction about OTE level—one claim puts it at 50%, another at 61.8%. This qualifies as a valid research question with proper grounding. By contrast, wondering whether FVGs work better on Tuesdays lacks any basis in existing claims or evidence, so it's mere speculation rather than a research question. A gap in the current interpretation—say, silence on which timeframes make FVGs most reliable—does provide substantive grounding for asking that question.

When operationalizing a concept reveals ambiguity about how to apply it in a specific case, that limitation becomes grounds for investigating the edge case. Not every variation across sources automatically warrants investigation; the researcher decides whether the variation itself settles the matter or whether deeper inquiry is needed. Similarly, interpretive tensions between defensible claims may or may not prompt research depending on whether resolution seems worth pursuing. An empirical anomaly—where test results contradict what the interpretation predicts—also signals a potential research question.

By contrast, if the interpretation already answers the question being asked, there's no genuine gap to investigate. Questions outside the research domain, like technical implementation details unrelated to trading concepts or market behavior, fall outside scope. However, questions about whether a source actually contains what's attributed to it remain valid when they affect the reliability of claims being examined.

The framework settles on researcher-identified questions grounded in substantive conditions—contradictions, gaps, tensions, limitations, or anomalies within the research state itself. These must be pertinent to the domain and genuinely worth investigating, distinct from simply answering existing claims or forming hypotheses. This establishes what feeds into hypothesis formation and operationalization downstream. The remaining architectural questions about documentation and storage can be addressed separately. # DECISION B1 — RESEARCH-QUESTION IDENTIFICATION

**Status:** PROPOSED — awaiting semantic consistency audit
**Upstream Authority:**
1. PROJECT MINORE — FOUNDING DEFINITION v0.1
2. DECISION D1 — CONCEPT UNDERSTANDING: RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION LIFECYCLE
3. DECISION A1 — RESEARCH INPUT ADMISSIBILITY AND PROVENANCE
4. DECISION A2 — CLAIM EXTRACTION PROCEDURE
5. A2-C1 and A2-C2 (BINDING)
6. DECISION A3 — CLAIM CLASSIFICATION
7. A3-C1 (BINDING)
8. DECISION A4 — CONCEPT ASSOCIATION
9. DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE
10. DECISION A6 — RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION CONSTRUCTION
11. DECISION A7 — INTERPRETATION EVOLUTION TRIGGERS
12. A7-C1 and A7-C2 (BINDING)

---

## 1. Exact Semantic Distinctions

| Term | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| **Research question** | A substantively grounded question about the research domain that the researcher identifies as worth investigating, arising from gaps, uncertainties, contradictions, limitations, or well-founded inquiry in the current research state. |
| **Source claim** | What a source said (A1–A2). Immutable and attributable (D1). Research questions may arise from claims but are not themselves claims. |
| **Researcher interpretation** | The current authoritative understanding of a concept (A6). Research questions may identify what the interpretation does not adequately address, but identifying a question does not modify the interpretation. |
| **Unresolved contradiction** | Claims that cannot both be true simultaneously (A5), preserved without forced reconciliation. May be an origin for research questions but does not automatically become one. |
| **Research gap** | An aspect of the research domain where the current interpretation or available claim landscape provides insufficient, uncertain, incomplete, or absent understanding. |
| **Hypothesis** | A testable proposition formulated in response to a research question (later stages; not B1). Research questions precede hypotheses. |
| **Mere curiosity** | A wondering or speculative question that lacks substantive grounding in the research domain or current research state. |
| **Research-question origin** | The condition in the current research state (contradiction, gap, tension, limitation, anomaly, or grounded inquiry) that gives rise to identifying a research question. |
| **Question resolution** | Achieving sufficient understanding to consider a research question answered or settled. Resolution may occur through interpretation construction, empirical evidence, or further source discovery. |

---

## 2. Minimum Options

### Option A — Automatic Generation from Research-State Conditions
Every unresolved contradiction, gap, uncertainty, tension, variation, or anomaly automatically becomes a research question.

**Problem:** Creates unsustainable noise; treats all uncertainties as equally worthy of investigation; violates minimum-decision principle; removes researcher judgment; prevents prioritization or focus.

### Option B — Pure Researcher Designation
Only the researcher's explicit designation creates a research question, without requiring grounding in the research state.

**Problem:** Arbitrary; permits baseless speculation; no accountability to research domain or available evidence; disconnects questions from research state; undermines methodological rigor.

### Option C — Researcher-Identified Questions Grounded in Substantive Research-State Conditions
A research question is identified when:
- A substantive gap, uncertainty, contradiction, limitation, anomaly, or well-founded inquiry exists in the current research state
- The researcher judges it worth investigating
- It pertains to the research domain (A2-C1)

**Test against upstream:** Preserves researcher authority (D1) while requiring substantive grounding; maintains connection to research state (A5, A6); respects domain boundaries (A2-C1); does not force automatic question generation.

**Verdict:** Option C is the minimum viable and founding-consistent standard.

---

## 3. Research-Question Identification Procedure

The following procedure governs how the researcher identifies research questions from the current research state.

**Step 1 — Identify potential origins in the current research state**

Possible origins include:
- Unresolved genuine contradictions (A5)
- Acknowledged tensions or variations in the available claim landscape
- Explicit gaps or limitations acknowledged in the current interpretation (A6)
- Uncertain or provisional aspects of the current interpretation
- Empirical anomalies or unexpected results from prior testing
- Downstream departures (A6 Rule 12) that reveal interpretive ambiguities or limitations
- Researcher-generated inquiry grounded in the research domain and current research state

Not all of these automatically become research questions. They are potential origins.

**Step 2 — Assess substantive grounding**

The potential question must satisfy:
- **Domain relevance:** Does it pertain to trading knowledge, market behavior, price mechanics, trading methods, or concepts relevant to trading-model development (A2-C1)?
- **Research-state connection:** Does it address something not adequately answered by the current interpretation and available claim landscape?
- **Substantive basis:** Is it grounded in actual contradictions, gaps, tensions, limitations, or evidence rather than pure speculation?

**Step 3 — Researcher identification**

The researcher formally identifies the question as worth investigating. This is a judgment call constrained by substantive grounding but not mechanically determined.

The researcher may:
- Identify the question and pursue it
- Acknowledge a potential question exists but defer or decline investigation
- Refine, narrow, or broaden a question before pursuing it

**Step 4 — Preserve the question without forcing resolution**

Identifying a research question does not require:
- Immediate answering
- Hypothesis formation
- Test design
- Empirical operationalization
- Interpretation modification

Research questions may remain open indefinitely.

---

## 4. Binding Methodology Rules

1. **A research question is substantively grounded in the current research state.** Not every contradiction, gap, uncertainty, tension, variation, anomaly, or curiosity automatically becomes a research question. The researcher identifies which are worth investigating based on substantive grounding and judgment.

2. **Research-question identification is distinct from answering the question.** Identifying a question does not require knowing the answer, having a hypothesis, having a test design, or having empirical results. B1 concerns identification; later stages address answering.

3. **Research questions may arise from multiple origins:** Unresolved contradictions (A5), acknowledged tensions or variations, gaps or limitations in the current interpretation (A6), uncertain or provisional interpretations, empirical anomalies, downstream departures revealing issues, or researcher-generated inquiry grounded in the research domain and current research state.

4. **A research question need not be immediately testable or empirically operationalizable at B1.** Empirical testability is addressed in later stages. Some research questions may be conceptual, definitional, or structural rather than empirical.

5. **Identifying a research question does not modify the current authoritative interpretation (A6).** The interpretation remains authoritative until explicitly revised. The question identifies what requires further investigation without changing what the researcher currently believes.

6. **Identifying a research question does not automatically trigger interpretation reconsideration under A7.** Research-question identification and A7 reconsideration triggers are distinct processes with different conditions. A research question may exist without requiring interpretation reconsideration, and a reconsideration trigger may exist without generating a research question.

7. **Research questions may remain open, be refined, narrowed, broadened, combined, split, or retired without being answered.** No mandatory resolution timeline or closure requirement exists.

8. **The researcher has authority to identify research questions.** This authority is constrained by substantive grounding in the research state and domain relevance, but within those constraints the researcher exercises judgment about what is worth investigating.

9. **Research questions must pertain to the research domain.** Per A2-C1, the domain is trading knowledge, market behavior, price mechanics, trading methods, or concepts relevant to trading-model development. Questions outside this domain are not Project Minore research questions.

10. **A question already adequately answered by the current interpretation and available claim landscape is not a valid research question.** If the interpretation already provides a settled answer without acknowledged gaps or limitations, identifying it as an open research question contradicts the interpretation's authoritative status.

---

## 5. Adversarial Tests

**Test 1 — Unresolved genuine contradiction as origin**

Current state: Two claims about Optimal Trade Entry level (50% vs. 61.8%) classified by A5 as Genuine Contradiction (unresolved). Current interpretation acknowledges the contradiction but provisionally adopts a position.

Potential research question: *"What is the empirically optimal retracement level for Optimal Trade Entry?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes (trading method)
- Grounding: Genuine unresolved contradiction in foundational claims
- Substantive basis: Yes
- Researcher judgment: If the researcher identifies this as worth investigating → Valid research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Unresolved contradiction provides substantive origin; researcher authority determines whether to pursue

---

**Test 2 — Mere curiosity with no grounding**

Speculative wondering: *"Do Fair Value Gaps work better on Tuesdays than Wednesdays?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes (market behavior)
- Grounding in current research state: No claim, interpretation, contradiction, or evidence addresses day-of-week effects
- Substantive basis: Pure speculation without grounding

**Classification:** Mere curiosity, not a valid research question at this stage. Could become valid if future claims or evidence introduce day-of-week considerations into the research state.

**Framework handling:** ✓ Lacks substantive grounding; correctly excluded

---

**Test 3 — Gap in current interpretation**

Current interpretation: Defines Fair Value Gap structure but is silent on timeframe applicability (e.g., does FVG identification work the same on 1-minute vs. daily charts?).

Potential research question: *"On which timeframes are Fair Value Gaps reliably identifiable and behaviorally consistent?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Grounding: Explicit gap in current interpretation (timeframe scope unaddressed)
- Substantive basis: Yes (addresses limitation)
- If researcher identifies as worth investigating → Valid research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Gap in interpretation provides substantive origin

---

**Test 4 — Downstream departure revealing interpretive limitation**

Researcher attempts to operationalize Order Block identification for testing. Discovery: the interpretation is ambiguous about whether overlapping consolidations count as one block or multiple blocks.

Potential research question: *"How should overlapping consolidation structures be classified in Order Block identification?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Grounding: Interpretive ambiguity revealed by operationalization attempt (downstream departure)
- Substantive basis: Yes
- Valid research question origin

**Framework handling:** ✓ Downstream departure revealing limitation is valid origin per Rule 3

---

**Test 5 — Minor variation not worth investigating**

Three sources describe slight timing variations (8:30 vs. 8:32 vs. 8:35) for the same session event within the same general concept.

Researcher assessment: The variation exists but is not material; the concept is adequately understood as "around 8:30."

- Potential question exists: "What is the exact optimal timing?"
- Researcher judgment: Not worth investigating; settled by "approximate timing" understanding
- No research question formally identified

**Framework handling:** ✓ Not every variation becomes a question; researcher authority applies

---

**Test 6 — Tension without clear investigative direction**

Two claims create interpretive tension about Market Maker intent (intentional vs. side-effect). Current interpretation brackets the contradiction.

- Potential question: *"Is Market Maker stop-loss hunting intentional or a side effect?"*
- Grounding: Yes (tension in available claim landscape)
- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Researcher may or may not identify this as worth investigating depending on whether intent affects trading-model construction
- If identified → Valid research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Researcher authority determines whether to pursue

---

**Test 7 — Empirical anomaly**

Prior backtest produced results contradicting a prediction embedded in the current interpretation (e.g., interpretation predicted FVGs fill 80%+ of the time; test showed 45%).

Potential research question: *"Under what conditions do Fair Value Gaps fill, and why did the test contradict the interpretation's prediction?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Grounding: Empirical anomaly
- Substantive basis: Yes (contradiction between interpretation and evidence)
- Valid research question origin

**Framework handling:** ✓ Empirical anomaly is valid origin per Rule 3

---

**Test 8 — Question already answered by current interpretation**

Question: *"What is a Fair Value Gap?"*

Current interpretation: Provides complete definition with structural, mechanistic, and predictive aspects.

- Domain-relevant: Yes
- Already answered: Yes, by current authoritative interpretation
- No gap, uncertainty, or limitation acknowledged
- Not a valid research question (settled)

**Framework handling:** ✓ Rule 10 excludes already-answered questions

---

**Test 9 — Question not pertaining to research domain**

Question: *"How do I code a Python backtest engine?"*

- Not about trading knowledge, market behavior, price mechanics, or trading methods
- Outside research domain per A2-C1 (technical implementation, not trading knowledge)
- Not a valid Project Minore research question

**Framework handling:** ✓ Rule 9 enforces domain boundary

---

**Test 10 — Research question about source provenance**

A claim's provenance is uncertain (third-party transcription with low confidence; A1). Question: *"Did the original source actually assert this, or is the transcription in error?"*

- Domain-relevant: Yes (affects claim reliability and interpretation foundation)
- Grounding: Provenance uncertainty (A1 Rule 3)
- Substantive basis: Yes (uncertainty about what source actually said)
- Valid research question origin

**Framework handling:** ✓ Provenance uncertainty is research-relevant and valid origin

---

## 6. Recommended Decision

**Option C — Researcher-Identified Questions Grounded in Substantive Research-State Conditions**

B1 establishes that research questions are:
- Substantively grounded in the current research state (contradictions, gaps, tensions, limitations, anomalies, or well-founded inquiry)
- Pertinent to the research domain (A2-C1 boundary)
- Identified by the researcher as worth investigating (researcher authority with substantive constraint)
- Distinct from answering, hypothesis formation, testing, or interpretation modification

Not every contradiction, gap, uncertainty, or curiosity becomes a research question. The researcher identifies which are worth pursuing based on substantive grounding and judgment.

---

## 7. What B1 Unlocks

According to the ratified dependency structure and Founding Definition mission pipeline:

- **M2 — Hypothesis Formation and Operationalization** (from the Research Methodology Decision Decomposition) is the next stage after B1. Research questions provide the input for hypothesis formation. M2 determines what makes a research question testable and how to formulate falsifiable hypotheses.

- Later stages (M3 — Experimental Rigor and Evidence Assessment, empirical testing, evidence evaluation) can now reference identified research questions as the origin of empirical work.

---

| Question | Classification |
|----------|---------------|
| How research questions are documented, stored, or tracked | DEFER TO ARCHITECTURE |
| Whether research questions have statuses, lifecycle states, or workflows | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS |
| Prioritization or sequencing of research questions | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS |
| Whether AI assists in identifying research questions | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS |
| Criteria for formally retiring or resolving research questions | Methodology content — not blocking M2 |
| Whether research questions have confidence levels or uncertainty metrics | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS |

---

## 9. Final Verdict

**DECISION B1 PROPOSED.**

Research-question identification model: Researcher-identified questions grounded in substantive research-state conditions (contradictions, gaps, tensions, limitations, anomalies, or grounded inquiry within the research domain).

Preserves researcher authority while requiring substantive grounding in the current research state. Maintains epistemic boundaries between question identification and answering. Does not modify the current authoritative interpretation or automatically trigger A7 reconsideration. Does not force automatic question generation from every research-state condition.

No contradiction with Founding Definition v0.1 or any ratified upstream decision detected.

**M2 — Hypothesis Formation and Operationalization is now unblocked.**

DECISION A7 — ADDITIONAL BINDING CLARIFICATIONS
Upstream Authority:

DECISION A7 — INTERPRETATION EVOLUTION TRIGGERS (RATIFIED)
Post-Ratification Semantic Consistency Verification — Decision A7
A7-C1 — PERMISSIVE / INSUFFICIENT TRIGGER BOUNDARY
The following binding distinction supersedes any A7 wording that implies "relevant to the concept but non-foundational → automatically Permissive."

Corrected Three-Category Definitions
Mandatory: The change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation.

Permissive: The change is relevant to the concept or interpretation, provides some substantive basis for possible reconsideration, and does not materially affect the documented foundation. The researcher may reconsider but is not required to. Changes meeting this threshold must be acknowledged in the research record.

Insufficient: The event provides no substantive basis for reconsideration. This includes preference shifts, silent substitutions, downstream departures without substantive findings, and changes that are nominally relevant to the concept but contain no new information, reasoning, claim, contextual implication, support-landscape change, or substantive finding not already fully accounted for in the available claim landscape.

Binding Clarifications
Relevance to the concept alone is not sufficient to create a Permissive trigger.

A Permissive trigger must provide some new substantive basis for possible reconsideration, however limited.

A change that is nominally relevant but contains no new information, reasoning, claim, contextual implication, support-landscape change, or substantive finding is Insufficient.

Relevant non-foundational changes that satisfy the Permissive threshold must be acknowledged in the research record. Reconsideration is not required.

This clarification supersedes any A7 wording that implies: "relevant to the concept but non-foundational → automatically Permissive."

Corrected Step 2 Decision Rule
If the change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation → Mandatory
If the change does not materially affect the documented foundation but is relevant and provides some substantive basis for possible reconsideration → Permissive
If the event provides no substantive basis for reconsideration → Insufficient
A7-C2 — DUPLICATION VS. INDEPENDENT CORROBORATION
The following binding distinction corrects former A7 Test 2, which classified all duplicate supporting claims as Permissive without distinguishing substantive duplication types.

Binding Distinctions
1. Exact or substantively redundant duplication

A claim repeats content already fully represented in the available claim landscape. It adds no new information, reasoning, contextual implication, or meaningful change to the support landscape.

Trigger classification: Insufficient.

2. Independent corroboration

A distinct source independently reaches or supports the same conclusion. This changes the support landscape by adding independent convergence, which is research-relevant even when the conclusion is the same.

Trigger classification: Permissive, unless the independent corroboration materially affects the documented interpretation foundation, in which case the general A7 Trigger Determination Framework (Step 2) determines whether it is Mandatory.

Semantic constraint: A separate source alone is not sufficient to establish independent corroboration. The support must be genuinely independent rather than derivative, copied, transcribed, or otherwise non-independent in origin. No full independence-assessment procedure is defined here; this remains a semantic distinction for researcher judgment.

3. Relevant non-foundational extension

A new claim adds substantive information relevant to the concept without materially affecting the documented interpretation foundation.

Trigger classification: Permissive.

Corrected Test 2
Concept: Fair Value Gap
New claim: A second source states the same three-candle imbalance definition already fully present in the available claim landscape from Source A.

Sub-case determination:

If the second source is derivative, transcribed, or copied from Source A: Insufficient — exact/substantively redundant duplication; no new information, no support-landscape change.
If the second source independently developed or arrived at the same conclusion: Permissive — independent corroboration; the support landscape changes by adding convergent independent evidence, which is research-relevant.
In either case, the documented interpretation foundation is not materially affected; no Mandatory trigger arises.

Ratification Effect
Decision A7 remains RATIFIED.
A7-C1 and A7-C2 are binding clarifications.
They supersede conflicting wording in A7 concerning the Permissive/Insufficient boundary and duplicate supporting claims.
No other part of A7 changes.
A7's materiality-based trigger model remains unchanged.
B1 — Research-Question Identification is now unblocked.

POST-RATIFICATION SEMANTIC CONSISTENCY VERIFICATION — DECISION A7
Upstream Authority: All ratified decisions through A7 inclusive.

Verification 1 — Mandatory / Permissive / Insufficient: Exhaustiveness and Exclusivity
Exact tension:

Step 2 classifies:

Foundation-affecting change → Mandatory
Relevant to concept but not foundation-affecting → Permissive
No substantive basis → Insufficient
The boundary between Permissive and Insufficient rests on "relevant to the concept." But relevance alone may not be sufficient to create a substantive basis for reconsideration. A change could be nominally relevant (e.g., a claim about a concept the interpretation addresses) without providing any genuine grounds for questioning the interpretation.

Upstream authority test:

A6 prohibits silent dismissal of materially relevant claims. A7 must not create a loophole where nominally relevant but substantively empty changes require research-record acknowledgment and classification overhead without any real research value.

Adversarial test:

A new claim states: "Fair Value Gaps exist." The interpretation already defines FVGs extensively. The new claim is technically relevant to the concept but provides no substantive basis for any reconsideration — it is neither conflicting, extending, qualifying, nor independently corroborating in a meaningful way.

Under current A7, this would be classified as Permissive (relevant to concept). Under Insufficient (no substantive basis), it arguably belongs there.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes, narrow. "Relevant to the concept" is too broad as the sole Permissive criterion. The correct Permissive criterion requires that the change be relevant AND provide some substantive basis for possible reconsideration — however minimal. A purely tautological or vacuous claim that happens to mention the concept is Insufficient, not Permissive.

Minimum correction required:

Clarify Step 2 and Rule structure:

"Permissive: the change is relevant to the concept or interpretation and provides some substantive basis for reconsideration, but does not materially affect the documented foundation."

"Insufficient: the event provides no substantive basis for reconsideration, including changes that are nominally relevant to the concept but contain no new information, reasoning, or claim not already fully accounted for in the available claim landscape."

Classification: NARROW CLARIFICATION

Verification 2 — Duplicate Supporting Claims (Test 2)
Exact tension:

Test 2 classifies a new claim that exactly duplicates existing support as Permissive ("adds support but does not affect the foundation's meaning or stability").

But the Insufficient definition states: "provides no substantive basis for reconsideration."

An exact duplicate adds nothing new. It does not change the available claim landscape in any substantive way. It provides no grounds for reconsideration.

Adversarial test:

Three sub-cases must be distinguished:

(a) Exact/substantively redundant duplication: Source B states word-for-word or near-identical content already in the available claim landscape from Source A. This provides no new information. The claim landscape is unchanged in substance. → Insufficient.

(b) Independent corroboration: A different source, independently, reaches the same conclusion. This changes the support landscape — the same conclusion is now supported by two independent sources. This may be research-relevant (convergence). → Permissive.

(c) Relevant non-foundational extension: A new claim extends the concept in a non-foundational direction. → Permissive.

Upstream authority test:

A2 (Test 9) establishes that repeated claims across source locations are extracted separately because "source repetition is a research-relevant fact." However, A2's rationale is provenance preservation during extraction, not trigger classification. The question for A7 is different: does independent corroboration provide a substantive basis for reconsideration?

For A7's purposes: independent corroboration strengthens (or weakens) the available evidence base for the interpretation, which may matter. Exact duplication within the same source adds nothing.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes. Test 2 classifies all duplication as Permissive without distinguishing exact/redundant duplication (Insufficient) from independent corroboration (Permissive). This allows vacuous triggers through the Permissive gate.

Minimum correction required:

Revise Test 2 to distinguish:

Exact or substantively redundant duplication → Insufficient (no new substantive basis)
Independent corroboration from a separate source → Permissive (changes support landscape; may be research-relevant)
Classification: NARROW CLARIFICATION

Verification 3 — Step 2 Decision Rule vs. Binding Rules and Tests
Exact tension:

Step 2 states: "No, but change is relevant to concept → Permissive reconsideration trigger."

Unified test: apply this rule to every adversarial test.

Test 1 (conflicting claim, foundation-affecting) → Mandatory ✓
Test 2 (duplicate support) → Step 2 says Permissive; Verification 2 above finds this may be Insufficient ✗
Test 3 (new preserved context, foundation-affecting) → Mandatory ✓
Test 4 (A5 status change, foundation-affecting) → Mandatory ✓
Test 5 (empirical results, foundation-affecting) → Mandatory ✓
Test 6 (researcher re-evaluation, unchanged outputs) → Permissive ✓
Test 7 (preference shift) → Insufficient ✓
Test 8 (downstream departure) → Not a trigger by itself ✓
Test 9 (peripheral new claim) → Permissive ✓
Test 10 (A3 reclassification, non-foundational) → Permissive ✓
Whether a real inconsistency exists:

One inconsistency: Test 2 (exact duplication classified as Permissive) conflicts with the Insufficient definition ("no substantive basis"). This is the same finding as Verification 2 — no new inconsistency beyond that.

The broader Step 2 rule ("relevant to concept → Permissive") is too broad as confirmed by Verification 1. Correction of Verifications 1 and 2 resolves this.

Minimum correction required:

Same as Verifications 1 and 2. No additional correction.

Classification: NO CORRECTION REQUIRED (covered by Verifications 1 and 2)

Verification 4 — Empirical Result Trigger Consistency
Exact tension:

A7 Rule 8: "New empirical results that bear materially on claims the current interpretation explicitly relies on as its documented foundation are a mandatory reconsideration trigger. A7 does not assess empirical evidence strength or quality."

Test whether A7 accidentally implies every empirical result is mandatory regardless of foundation relevance.

Adversarial test:

Empirical result: a backtest on a concept unrelated to the current interpretation's foundation (e.g., a study of session timing for a concept whose interpretation concerns only structural definition). Does this trigger mandatory reconsideration?

Under Rule 8: only if results "bear materially on claims the current interpretation explicitly relies on as its documented foundation." A result on an unrelated concept does not.

Does A7 assess evidence quality? No. The trigger condition is foundation materiality, not evidence quality. This is consistent.

Does A7 depend on M3 for trigger classification? No. Foundation materiality is assessed against the A6 documented foundation record, not against M3 criteria.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

No. Rule 8 correctly limits empirical triggers to foundation-affecting results without assessing evidence quality. No inadvertent generalization to all empirical results. No M3 dependency.

Classification: NO CORRECTION REQUIRED

Verification 5 — Mandatory Trigger Timing vs. Interpretation Remains Authoritative
Exact tension:

Rule 2: "The researcher must account for the trigger before relying on the current interpretation for downstream decisions that depend on the affected foundation."

Rule 1: "The current interpretation remains the authoritative reference interpretation until explicitly revised."

Test whether these conflict: if the researcher must account for the trigger before relying on the interpretation, but the interpretation remains authoritative until revised, what is the interpretation's status between trigger and reconsideration?

Adversarial test:

Mandatory trigger fires. Researcher has not yet performed reconsideration. A downstream decision arises that depends on the affected foundation.

Rule 2 says: account for the trigger before relying on the interpretation for that decision.
Rule 1 says: interpretation remains authoritative until explicitly revised.

These are not contradictory. The interpretation remains authoritative (it has not been revised). However, the researcher must account for the trigger before using it as a reference for the specific dependent downstream decision. "Accounting for" does not require revision — it requires acknowledging the trigger and reasoning about whether the interpretation remains sound.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

No. The two rules are coherent. The interpretation remains authoritative throughout; the mandatory trigger creates a processing obligation, not a suspension of authority. No contradiction.

Classification: NO CORRECTION REQUIRED

Verification 6 — A3 / A4 / A5 Authority Inheritance
Exact tension:

Rules 5, 6, 7: A7 inherits documented changes from A3, A4, A5 as delivered without re-performing upstream determinations.

Test whether A7 evaluates only the material impact of documented changes on the interpretation foundation, not the validity of the changes themselves.

Adversarial test:

A4 reassociates a claim with a concept. The reassociation may be in error. Does A7 validate the reassociation before treating it as a trigger?

Under Rules 6 and 7: A7 inherits the documented change as delivered. A7 then evaluates whether the reassociation materially affects the interpretation foundation. A7 does not re-perform the association analysis.

This is correct. If the A4 change is erroneous, the correction belongs at A4, not A7.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

No. Inheritance is correctly stated and applied. A7 evaluates foundation impact only.

Classification: NO CORRECTION REQUIRED

Verification 7 — Downstream Departure Boundary
Exact tension:

Rule 11: "Downstream departure does not itself trigger reconsideration. If a downstream departure reveals a substantive incompatibility or limitation in the current interpretation, that substantive finding may be a permissive trigger. The trigger is the finding, not the departure."

Test whether the framework correctly escalates: if a substantive finding from departure later materially affects the documented foundation, does A7 correctly classify it as Mandatory?

Adversarial test:

Step 1: Departure occurs. Not a trigger.
Step 2: Departure reveals substantive incompatibility with the foundation. → Permissive trigger (the finding).
Step 3: The finding, on evaluation, materially affects the documented interpretation foundation. → Step 2 of A7's Trigger Determination Framework reclassifies it as Mandatory.

Does A7 support this escalation?

Step 2 of the Trigger Determination Framework asks: does the change affect the documented foundation? The "change" in this case is the substantive finding. If the finding materially affects the foundation → Mandatory. If not → Permissive.

The framework handles this correctly through its own Step 2 logic. No gap exists.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

No. The escalation path from departure → finding → Permissive or Mandatory is handled by the general trigger determination framework without requiring a special rule.

Classification: NO CORRECTION REQUIRED

Minimum Corrections Required
Correction 1 — Permissive/Insufficient Boundary (Verifications 1 and 3)

Revise Step 2 and the Permissive/Insufficient definitions:

"Permissive: the change is relevant to the concept or interpretation, provides some substantive basis for possible reconsideration, and does not materially affect the documented foundation. The researcher may reconsider but is not required to; the change must be acknowledged in the research record."

"Insufficient: the event provides no substantive basis for reconsideration. This includes preference shifts, silent substitutions, downstream departures without substantive findings, and changes that are nominally relevant to the concept but contain no new information, reasoning, or claim not already fully accounted for in the available claim landscape."

Correction 2 — Duplicate Claims (Verification 2)

Revise Test 2:

"New claim — exact/substantively redundant duplication: a new claim repeats content already fully present in the available claim landscape from the same or another source, adding no new information. → Insufficient (no new substantive basis for reconsideration)."

"New claim — independent corroboration: a distinct source independently reaches the same conclusion. This changes the support landscape and may be research-relevant (convergence). → Permissive."

Final Verdict
B. A7 REMAINS RATIFIED WITH NARROW BINDING CLARIFICATIONS

Two narrow clarifications are required:

Permissive/Insufficient boundary requires explicit substantive-basis requirement in the Permissive definition
Test 2 requires distinction between exact/redundant duplication (Insufficient) and independent corroboration (Permissive)
Neither finding is a blocking defect. A7's core decision — the materiality-based trigger model — is sound, internally consistent, and correctly authorized by upstream decisions.
