# DECISION A7 — INTERPRETATION EVOLUTION TRIGGERS

**Status:** RATIFIED
**Upstream Authority:**
1. PROJECT MINORE — FOUNDING DEFINITION v0.1
2. DECISION D1 — CONCEPT UNDERSTANDING: RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION LIFECYCLE
3. DECISION A1 — RESEARCH INPUT ADMISSIBILITY AND PROVENANCE
4. DECISION A2 — CLAIM EXTRACTION PROCEDURE
5. A2-C1 and A2-C2 (BINDING)
6. DECISION A3 — CLAIM CLASSIFICATION
7. A3-C1 (BINDING)
8. DECISION A4 — CONCEPT ASSOCIATION
9. DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE
10. DECISION A6 — RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION CONSTRUCTION

---

## 1. Exact Semantic Distinctions

| Term | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| **Current interpretation** | The current authoritative reference interpretation of a concept (D1; A6). Remains authoritative until explicitly revised. |
| **Documented interpretation foundation** | The explicit set of claims, preserved context, and reasoning that the researcher identified as foundational when constructing the current interpretation under A6 Step 3. |
| **Reconsideration** | The act of re-evaluating whether the current interpretation remains justified given a change in one or more upstream outputs. Reconsideration is not interpretation revision. |
| **Interpretation evolution** | A change from the current interpretation to a revised interpretation. A7 governs when reconsideration is required or permitted, not how revision is performed. |
| **Trigger** | A change in one or more upstream outputs that justifies or requires reconsideration of the current interpretation. |
| **Mandatory trigger** | A trigger that requires the researcher to account for the change before relying on the current interpretation for downstream decisions that depend on the affected foundation. Reconsideration is required; evolution is not automatic. |
| **Permissive trigger** | A trigger that allows reconsideration but does not require it. Relevant non-foundational changes must be acknowledged in the research record but do not require reconsideration of the interpretation. |
| **Insufficient trigger** | An event that provides no substantive basis for reconsideration. |
| **Materiality to interpretation foundation** | Whether a change affects the claims, preserved context, contradiction statuses, or concept associations that the current interpretation explicitly relied on as its documented foundation (A6). |
| **Downstream departure** | A downstream research step that explicitly departs from the current interpretation for a specific task without changing the interpretation itself (A6 Rule 12). |

---

## 2. Minimum Options

### Option A — Any change triggers reconsideration
Any modification in any upstream output requires reconsideration.

**Problem:** Creates procedural paralysis; does not distinguish material from immaterial changes; inconsistent with the minimum-decision principle and the stability of the authoritative reference interpretation.

### Option B — Only new source claims trigger reconsideration
Reconsideration is justified only when a new claim is added to the available claim landscape.

**Problem:** Too narrow; ignores changes to preserved context, contradiction status, concept association, and empirical results — all of which can materially alter the basis of the current interpretation.

### Option C — Material-change trigger model (foundation-sensitive)
Reconsideration is required or permitted based on whether a change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation or is relevant to the concept without affecting the foundation.

- **Mandatory:** change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation
- **Permissive:** change is relevant to the concept or interpretation but does not materially affect the documented foundation
- **Insufficient:** event provides no substantive basis for reconsideration

**Verdict:** Option C is the minimum coherent model consistent with D1 (evolution + provenance), A6 (explicit foundation + authoritative reference interpretation), and the minimum-decision principle.

---

## 3. Trigger Determination Framework

A7 defines when reconsideration is required, permitted, or not justified, based on changes to upstream outputs.

### Step 1 — Identify the change

Possible change types:
- New source claim becomes available in the available claim landscape (A1–A2; A6)
- Newly discovered preserved source context affecting an existing claim (A2 / A3-C1)
- Documented change in claim classification or classification uncertainty (A3 / A3-C1) — inherited as delivered, not re-performed
- Documented change in concept association (A4) — inherited as delivered, not re-performed
- Documented change in A5 contradiction status (A5) — inherited as delivered
- New empirical results from later stages bearing on foundational claims
- Researcher re-evaluation of unchanged upstream outputs
- Preference change without substantive reasoning
- Downstream departure from the current interpretation

### Step 2 — Determine materiality to the documented interpretation foundation

Does the change affect the claims, preserved context, contradiction statuses, or concept associations that the current interpretation explicitly relied on as its documented foundation (A6)?

- **Yes → Mandatory reconsideration trigger**
- **No, but change is relevant to the concept → Permissive reconsideration trigger; acknowledge in research record**
- **No substantive basis → Insufficient trigger**

Note: Material relevance to the concept is not equivalent to material impact on the documented interpretation foundation. A change may be relevant without destabilizing the foundation.

### Step 3 — Classify the trigger

- **Mandatory:** The change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation. The researcher must account for the trigger before relying on the current interpretation for downstream decisions that depend on the affected foundation. No immediate review-session requirement or backlog obligation is implied.

- **Permissive:** The change is relevant to the concept or interpretation but does not materially affect the documented foundation. The researcher may reconsider but is not required to. A6's prohibition on silent dismissal is satisfied by acknowledging the change in the research record.

- **Insufficient:** The event provides no substantive basis for reconsideration. The current interpretation remains authoritative without engagement.

---

## 4. Binding Methodology Rules

1. **The current interpretation remains the authoritative reference interpretation until explicitly revised** (D1; A6). A7 defines when reconsideration is required or permitted; it does not itself revise the interpretation.

2. **Mandatory reconsideration is foundation-sensitive.** If a change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation, the researcher must account for the trigger before relying on the current interpretation for downstream decisions that depend on the affected foundation. No periodic review schedule or backlog obligation is implied.

3. **New materially relevant conflicting claims in the available claim landscape require reconsideration, not automatic revision.** A new claim conflicting with the current interpretation and materially affecting the foundation triggers mandatory reconsideration. The interpretation may remain unchanged after reconsideration with explicit reasoning.

4. **Newly discovered preserved source context affecting a foundational claim is a mandatory trigger.** If new preserved context changes the meaning, scope, or applicability of a claim the interpretation explicitly relied on, reconsideration is required.

5. **Documented changes in claim classification or classification uncertainty are mandatory triggers when they materially affect the interpretation foundation.** A7 inherits these changes from A3/A3-C1 as delivered without re-performing classification determinations.

6. **Documented changes in concept association are mandatory triggers when they materially affect the interpretation foundation.** A7 inherits these changes from A4 as delivered without re-performing association determinations.

7. **Documented changes in A5 contradiction status are mandatory triggers when they involve foundational claims.** If the contradiction landscape relevant to the documented foundation changes, reconsideration is required. A7 inherits A5 contradiction status as delivered.

8. **New empirical results that bear materially on claims the current interpretation explicitly relies on as its documented foundation are a mandatory reconsideration trigger.** A7 does not assess empirical evidence strength or quality. Whether a result justifies interpretation evolution is determined through reconsideration under A6's reasoning requirements and the future M3 evidential framework.

9. **Researcher re-evaluation without a documented upstream change is permitted, not mandatory.** The researcher may reconsider based on new reasoning applied to unchanged upstream outputs, but such reconsideration must be grounded in explicit reasoning (A6) and is not forced by A7.

10. **Preference change without substantive reasoning is insufficient.** A mere preference shift does not justify reconsideration or evolution.

11. **Downstream departure does not itself trigger reconsideration.** If a downstream departure reveals a substantive incompatibility or limitation in the current interpretation, that substantive finding may be a permissive trigger. The trigger is the finding, not the departure.

12. **No completeness requirement beyond the available claim landscape.** Triggers are evaluated against the bounded available claim landscape (A6). The existence of unknown or undiscovered material is not itself a trigger.

---

## 5. Adversarial Tests

**Test 1 — New materially relevant claim directly conflicts with current interpretation (Mandatory)**

Current interpretation: "Order Blocks require bearish consolidation before bullish impulses."
New claim (available claim landscape): "Order Blocks can be formed by any consolidation before any impulse direction."

- Change type: New claim in available claim landscape
- Materiality: Directly conflicts with the documented foundation's core definition
- Trigger: **Mandatory reconsideration**
- Outcome: Reconsideration required; evolution not automatic; interpretation may be retained with explicit reasoning

---

**Test 2 — New claim duplicates existing support (Permissive)**

Current interpretation foundation: "FVG is a three-candle imbalance" (supported by Claim A).
New claim: A second source repeats the same definition with similar scope.

- Change type: New claim in available claim landscape
- Materiality: Adds support but does not affect the foundation's meaning or stability
- Trigger: **Permissive reconsideration**
- Researcher must acknowledge the new claim in the research record; reconsideration not required

---

**Test 3 — Newly discovered preserved context changes meaning of a foundational claim (Mandatory)**

Foundational claim: "FVGs always fill."
Newly recovered preserved context: "...in non-trending conditions; in strong trends they may remain unfilled."

- Change type: Newly discovered preserved source context (A2 / A3-C1)
- Materiality: Directly modifies scope and applicability of a foundational claim
- Trigger: **Mandatory reconsideration**

---

**Test 4 — A5 reclassifies prior tension as genuine contradiction involving foundational claims (Mandatory)**

Previously: two claims about OTE level classified as Tension.
A5 update: after applying new preserved context, status becomes Genuine Contradiction.
Both claims are part of the documented interpretation foundation.

- Change type: Documented A5 contradiction status change — inherited from A5 as delivered
- Materiality: Contradiction landscape affecting documented foundation changed
- Trigger: **Mandatory reconsideration**

---

**Test 5 — New empirical results bearing materially on foundational claims (Mandatory)**

Current interpretation relies on: "FVGs tend to fill before continuation."
New empirical results: backtest results bearing on FVG fill behavior.

- Change type: New empirical results from later stages
- Materiality: Bears on claims the documented interpretation foundation explicitly relies on
- Trigger: **Mandatory reconsideration**
- A7 does not assess evidence strength or quality; whether results justify evolution is determined through reconsideration under A6 and future M3 framework

---

**Test 6 — Researcher changes mind after re-reading unchanged upstream outputs (Permissive)**

No new claims, no new preserved context, no A5 status change, no new empirical results.
Researcher re-reads the same available claim landscape and develops a better synthesis.

- Change type: Researcher re-evaluation of unchanged upstream outputs
- Trigger: **Permissive reconsideration** — must be grounded in explicit reasoning; not required by A7

---

**Test 7 — Preference change with no substantive reasoning (Insufficient)**

Researcher prefers a cleaner rule with no re-evaluation and no new upstream output.

- Change type: Preference shift only
- Trigger: **Insufficient**

---

**Test 8 — Downstream research explicitly departs without changing interpretation (Insufficient by itself)**

A hypothesis test temporarily assumes an alternative definition, explicitly noted as departure (A6 Rule 12).

- Change type: Downstream explicit departure
- Trigger: **Not a trigger by itself**
- If the departure reveals a substantive incompatibility or limitation in the current interpretation, that finding may be a **Permissive trigger**; the trigger is the finding, not the departure

---

**Test 9 — New material affects a peripheral aspect not in the interpretation foundation (Permissive)**

New claim concerns a niche session condition not referenced in the documented interpretation foundation.

- Change type: New claim in available claim landscape
- Materiality: Relevant to the concept; does not materially affect the documented foundation
- Trigger: **Permissive reconsideration**; must be acknowledged in research record

---

**Test 10 — A3 reclassification not affecting interpretation foundation (Permissive)**

A claim previously classified as Predictive is reclassified as Mechanistic.
The claim was not part of the documented interpretation foundation.

- Change type: Documented A3 classification change — inherited from A3 as delivered
- Materiality: Not foundation-affecting
- Trigger: **Permissive reconsideration**

---

## 6. Ratified Decision

**Option C — Material-change trigger model (foundation-sensitive)**

A7 adopts a trigger framework where reconsideration of the current interpretation is:

- **Mandatory** when a change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation (available claim landscape additions/changes, preserved context changes, A3 classification changes, A4 association changes, A5 contradiction status changes, or new empirical results — all assessed against the documented foundation)
- **Permitted** when relevant changes occur without materially affecting the documented foundation; relevant non-foundational changes must be acknowledged in the research record
- **Not justified** when only preference shifts, silent substitutions, or downstream departures (without substantive findings) are present

A7 distinguishes reconsideration from interpretation evolution and does not define the revision procedure. A7 does not assess empirical evidence strength or quality; that determination belongs to M3.

---

## 7. What A7 Unlocks

- **B1 — Research-Question Identification** is now directly unblocked. The methodology can now identify unanswered research questions within the established interpretation landscape, with a defined framework for when interpretations may evolve.
- Downstream research work (B1, M2, M3) can rely on a stable current interpretation with a principled, bounded account of when that interpretation must or may be reconsidered.

---

## 8. Remaining Questions — Classified Only

| Question | Classification |
|----------|---------------|
| Procedure for performing an interpretation revision once reconsideration is triggered | Methodology content — outside A7 scope |
| How to document and record trigger events and their linkage to interpretations | DEFER TO ARCHITECTURE |
| How to represent trigger acknowledgment in the research record | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS / Methodology detail |
| Whether empirical evidence strength determines if evolution is required after reconsideration | DEFER TO M3 |
| Whether periodic interpretation review is warranted in long-running research | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS |

---

- **Status:** RATIFIED
- **Ratification basis:** Revised Semantic Consistency Audit — six corrections applied
- **Corrections applied:**
  - Removed "available research state" as undefined composite term; replaced with specific references to established upstream outputs throughout
  - Removed trigger-level evidence-quality distinctions; replaced with single foundation-materiality criterion; merged former Tests 5 and 6 into one test (Test 5); stated explicitly that A7 does not assess evidence strength
  - Clarified Mandatory/Permissive boundary: Mandatory = foundation-affecting change; Permissive = relevant but non-foundational change (acknowledged in research record); confirmed material relevance to concept is not equivalent to material impact on documented foundation
  - Added explicit A3/A4 inheritance clarification to Rules 5 and 6: A7 inherits documented changes as delivered without re-performing upstream determinations
  - Corrected downstream-departure handling: departure does not trigger reconsideration; substantive finding arising from departure may be a permissive trigger

E. Correct downstream-departure handling:

Downstream departure does not itself trigger reconsideration.
If the departure reveals a substantive incompatibility or limitation in the current interpretation, that substantive finding may be a Permissive trigger.
The trigger is the finding, not the departure.
F. Clarify mandatory reconsideration timing:

A Mandatory trigger must be accounted for before the current interpretation is relied upon for new downstream decisions that depend on the affected foundation.
Do not create a periodic review schedule, immediate review-session requirement, or backlog lifecycle obligation.

REVISED AUDIT FINDINGS — DECISION A7
Scope: Revised Finding 2 and Finding 3 only. All other findings from the original audit are preserved unchanged.

Revised Finding 2 — Empirical Evidence Triggers Before M3
Exact tension:

A7 distinguishes between empirical evidence that "materially contradicts or undermines claims" (mandatory trigger) and "weak or inconclusive empirical results" (permissive trigger). This distinction requires assessing evidence quality before M3 has defined evidence assessment criteria.

The original audit proposed collapsing the mandatory/permissive distinction for empirical evidence and deferring it to M3. However, this creates a dependency problem: A7 precedes B1, M2, and M3 in the ratified dependency order, and cannot depend on future M3 criteria to complete its own trigger classification.

Re-audit using the corrected distinction:

Reconsideration is not interpretation revision. A mandatory reconsideration trigger requires the researcher to account for a development — it does not require the interpretation to change.

Given this, A7 can determine whether new empirical results require reconsideration based solely on whether they bear materially on the documented interpretation foundation, without assessing evidence strength.

This means:

Any new empirical result that bears materially on claims the interpretation explicitly relies on requires reconsideration.
No quality distinction (weak/strong/inconclusive) is needed at the trigger level.
M3 later determines what weight the evidence deserves and whether evolution is justified.
Necessity test:

Does A7 need to classify empirical evidence as weak, inconclusive, strong, or contradictory?

No. The trigger question is: does this empirical result bear materially on the documented foundation? If yes, reconsideration is required. What the researcher concludes from that reconsideration — retain, modify, or revise the interpretation — is governed by A6's reasoning requirements and M3's evidential framework. A7 is not responsible for either.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes, but the inconsistency is narrower than originally found. The problem is not mandatory vs. permissive per se — it is that A7 uses quality descriptors ("weak," "inconclusive," "materially contradicts," "undermines") that constitute evidence assessment. These descriptors must be removed. The single criterion is materiality to the documented foundation.

Minimum correction required:

Replace the current mandatory/permissive distinction for empirical evidence with a single rule:

"New empirical results that bear materially on claims the current interpretation explicitly relies on as its foundation are a mandatory reconsideration trigger. A7 does not assess evidence strength or quality. Whether the empirical result justifies interpretation evolution is determined through reconsideration under A6's reasoning requirements and M3's future evidential framework."

Remove quality-based language ("weak," "inconclusive," "materially contradicts," "undermines") from Step 3, Rule 8, and Tests 5 and 6.

Merge Tests 5 and 6 into one test that applies the single foundation-materiality criterion without distinguishing evidence quality.

Classification: CORRECTION REQUIRED

Revised Finding 3 — Mandatory / Permissive Boundary
Exact tension:

The original audit found that A7's "permissive" category risks blurring into "mandatory" because A6 prohibits silent dismissal of materially relevant claims — implying that any materially relevant change might always require reconsideration.

The original audit's correction conflated:

A claim being materially relevant to the concept, and
A change materially affecting the documented interpretation foundation.
These are not equivalent.

Re-audit using the corrected distinction:

A claim may be materially relevant to a concept without materially affecting the current interpretation foundation. Examples:

A new claim providing additional support for a position the foundation already relies on — relevant, but not foundation-destabilizing.
A new claim extending the concept into a domain the foundation did not address — relevant, but the foundation's core reasoning remains intact.
A new claim introducing a variation the foundation already accommodates — relevant, but not a material change to the foundation.
In these cases, A6's prohibition on silent dismissal applies: the researcher must account for the new claim. However, "accounting for" a new claim does not require reconsideration of the interpretation if the claim does not affect the foundation.

This preserves a coherent Mandatory / Permissive boundary:

Mandatory: The change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation — the claims, preserved context, contradiction landscape, or concept associations the interpretation explicitly relied on. The researcher must reconsider.
Permissive: The change is relevant to the concept or interpretation but does not materially affect the documented foundation. The researcher may reconsider but is not required to. A6's prohibition on silent dismissal is satisfied by the researcher acknowledging the new claim in the research record.
Insufficient: The event provides no substantive basis for reconsideration (preference shift, silent substitution attempt, or downstream departure not revealing a substantive finding).
Upstream authority test:

A6 prohibits silent dismissal of available materially relevant claims. This governs interpretation construction, not post-construction trigger classification. A6 requires the researcher to account for claims during construction; A7 governs when constructed interpretations are reconsidered. These are distinct acts. A7's permissive category does not violate A6 provided the researcher acknowledges non-foundational relevant changes in the research record rather than ignoring them entirely.

Necessity test:

Is the revised boundary coherent without hidden overlap?

Yes. The distinction is between foundation-impact (mandatory) and concept-relevance-without-foundation-impact (permissive). The categories are now internally coherent and non-overlapping. The boundary is defined by reference to the A6-documented interpretation foundation, which is explicit and traceable.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

The original inconsistency (blurring of mandatory/permissive) was a product of the original audit's conflation error. Under the corrected formulation, no real inconsistency exists in A7's mandatory/permissive model. The categories are semantically distinct and correctly grounded.

Minimum correction required:

Revise A7 Step 2 and the relevant rules to explicitly state the corrected boundary:

"Mandatory: the change materially affects the documented interpretation foundation. Permissive: the change is relevant to the concept but does not materially affect the documented foundation — the researcher must acknowledge the change in the research record but is not required to reconsider the interpretation. Insufficient: the event provides no substantive basis for reconsideration."

Remove the language that implied A6's prohibition on silent dismissal creates a mandatory reconsideration trigger for all materially relevant claims.

Classification: NARROW CLARIFICATION

DECISION A7 — INTERPRETATION EVOLUTION TRIGGERS (PROPOSED, under audit)
Finding 1 — "Available Research State" vs. A6's Bounded Claim Landscape
Exact tension:

A7 defines "available research state" as: "currently admitted and processed research inputs, extracted claims, preserved contexts, claim classifications, concept associations, and contradiction statuses."

This definition encompasses the full output of A1–A5 plus empirical results from later stages. It is broader than A6's "available claim landscape" (bounded by admitted/processed/associated claims).

A7 then uses "available research state" as the domain within which trigger materiality is assessed (Step 2, Rule 12).

Upstream authority test:

A6 establishes the bounded "available claim landscape" — what is currently admitted, extracted, and associated — as the working domain for interpretation construction. A6 does not introduce "available research state" as a term.

The Founding Definition uses "research state" implicitly (pipeline stages) but does not define it as a semantic unit.

A7 is generating a new composite term not grounded in any ratified definition.

Necessity test:

Does A7 need "available research state" specifically, or can it operate within each component's own established boundary?

A7 references changes across multiple stages (A1–A5 changes, empirical evidence). It could instead refer directly to changes in specific outputs: "changes in the available claim landscape," "changes in A5 status," "new empirical evidence," etc. A composite term is not necessary.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes. "Available research state" is an undefined composite term. It implicitly expands A6's bounded domain without ratified authority to do so.

Minimum correction required:

Remove "available research state" as a defined term and throughout A7. Replace all occurrences with specific references to their authoritative source: "available claim landscape" (A6), "A5 status," "preserved source context" (A2), "concept associations" (A4), "empirical evidence" (future stages). This eliminates an unratified composite term while preserving A7's substantive content.

Classification: CORRECTION REQUIRED

Finding 2 — Evidence-Strength Assessment Before M3
Exact tension:

A7's Trigger Determination Framework distinguishes:

"Empirical evidence that materially contradicts or undermines claims" → mandatory trigger
"Weak or inconclusive empirical results" → permissive trigger
Test 5 identifies new empirical results as a mandatory trigger. Test 6 classifies "mixed results with wide uncertainty" as permissive.

A7's Rule 8 states: "A7 does not decide whether evidence is strong enough to force evolution."

Upstream authority test:

M3 — Experimental Rigor and Evidence Assessment is not yet ratified. No upstream ratified decision defines "weak," "inconclusive," "materially contradicts," or "mixed results."

A7 is drawing distinctions between evidence quality levels using undefined criteria — exactly what M3 will define.

Necessity test:

Can A7 define an evidence-based trigger without knowing what constitutes sufficient evidence?

A7 needs to say only: "New empirical evidence bearing on foundational claims is a reconsideration trigger." It does not need to distinguish mandatory vs. permissive based on evidence strength (which is M3 territory).

The mandatory/permissive distinction for empirical evidence specifically requires an evidence-quality judgment that A7 does not have authority to make.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes. The mandatory vs. permissive distinction for empirical evidence is implicitly an evidence-strength assessment, contradicting the explicit disclaimer in Rule 8 and overreaching into M3 territory.

Minimum correction required:

Collapse the empirical evidence trigger into a single rule: "New empirical evidence bearing materially on claims the current interpretation depends on is a reconsideration trigger. Whether that trigger is mandatory or permissive depends on evidence assessment criteria established in M3. A7 does not determine evidence strength."

Remove the mandatory/permissive distinction for empirical evidence from Step 3 and Rules 8. Remove Tests 5 and 6 or consolidate them into one test that acknowledges the M3 dependency.

Classification: CORRECTION REQUIRED

Finding 3 — Internal Coherence of Mandatory / Permissive / Insufficient Model
Exact tension:

A7 establishes three trigger categories:

Mandatory: requires reconsideration
Permissive: allows reconsideration
Insufficient: does not justify reconsideration
The semantic function of "mandatory" is: the researcher must reconsider (but may retain the interpretation after reconsideration with explicit reasoning).

The semantic function of "permissive" is: the researcher may reconsider (but is not required to).

The semantic function of "insufficient" is: no justification for reconsideration exists.

Test of internal coherence:

The categories are semantically distinct:

Mandatory preserves researcher authority (evolution not forced) while requiring engagement
Permissive respects researcher judgment without requiring engagement
Insufficient prevents arbitrary shifts (preference, silent substitution)
However, "Permissive" creates an ambiguity: if reconsideration is permitted but not required after a non-foundational relevant change, can the researcher permanently ignore materially relevant new claims on a non-foundational peripheral aspect?

A6 Rule 2 states: "silent dismissal of available materially relevant claims is prohibited."

If a new claim is materially relevant, A6 prohibits silent dismissal, which may mean reconsideration is always required for materially relevant changes — making "permissive" a category without a clear, coherent domain.

Upstream authority test:

A6 prohibits silent dismissal of materially relevant claims. If any new claim is materially relevant, it must be accounted for. A7's permissive category may apply only to claims that are relevant but not materially relevant (a distinction A7 does not currently draw clearly).

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Partial. A7's trigger model is internally coherent for its intended purpose, but the "permissive" category risks blurring into "mandatory" for anything truly materially relevant (due to A6's prohibition). The boundary between mandatory and permissive is not clearly defined.

Minimum correction required:

Clarify that permissive triggers apply to claims/changes that are relevant but not materially relevant (i.e., do not bear on the interpretation foundation). Any change that is materially relevant triggers mandatory reconsideration per A6. Revise the Step 3 criteria to make this explicit.

Classification: NARROW CLARIFICATION

Finding 4 — "Materiality to Interpretation Foundation" as Grounded Judgment Standard
Exact tension:

A7's entire trigger model rests on "materiality to interpretation foundation." Step 2 asks: "Does the change affect claims, context, or contradiction statuses that the current interpretation explicitly relied on as its foundation?"

This is defined in terms of the A6 interpretation foundation (the explicit foundation documented in Step 3 of A6).

Upstream authority test:

A6 establishes that the interpretation foundation is explicit and reasoned (A6 Step 3, Rule 3). The interpretation foundation is a documented product of A6. Referring to it in A7 is therefore grounded.

Necessity test:

Is "materiality to interpretation foundation" defined clearly enough to be applied without hidden judgment?

Since A6 requires the foundation to be explicit, the question "does this change affect the foundation?" is answerable by reference to the A6 interpretation document itself. This is a well-grounded standard.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

No. Materiality to interpretation foundation is sufficiently grounded in A6's explicit foundation requirement. No hidden judgment standard is introduced.

Minimum correction required:

None substantively required. A narrow clarification may be added to confirm that materiality is assessed against the A6-documented interpretation foundation record, not against a new judgment standard.

Classification: NO CORRECTION REQUIRED

Finding 5 — Mandatory Trigger Based on Unvalidated Change
Exact tension:

A7 Rule 6 states: "Changes in concept association can be mandatory triggers if they add/remove substantively relevant claims from the concept's available claim landscape in a way that materially affects the interpretation foundation."

A4 governs concept association. A4 Rule 8 states concept association does not alter claims; it is metadata about subject matter.

A7 Rule 5 states: "Changes in claim classification or classification uncertainty can be mandatory triggers."

A3 governs classification. A3 Rule 2: classification must not alter the extracted claim.

Test:

If an A4 reassociation is in error (e.g., a claim is incorrectly newly associated with a concept), A7 would trigger mandatory reconsideration based on an incorrect association. Similarly, if an A3 reclassification is incorrect, A7 would trigger reconsideration based on an incorrect classification.

However: A7 is not reviewing the validity of A4 or A3 outputs. It is responding to them.

Upstream authority test:

A7 must inherit A4 and A3 outputs without re-performing them. Changes to concept association and classification are A4/A3 outputs, not A7 decisions. A7 responding to them is correct.

Necessity test:

Should A7 require reconsideration based on any A4/A3 change, or only validated ones?

A7 has no authority to validate A4/A3 outputs. If an association or classification changes, A7 responds to that change. The validity of A4/A3 output is an A4/A3 concern. A7 responding to changes is correct and does not validate them.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

No material inconsistency. A7 correctly inherits A3/A4 outputs. However, a narrow clarification would help: A7 responds to documented changes in A3/A4 status as delivered; it does not re-examine whether those changes were correctly made.

Minimum correction required:

Narrow clarification: Add a note in Rule 5 and Rule 6 that A7 responds to documented changes in classification or association as inherited from A3/A4 without re-performing those upstream determinations.

Classification: NARROW CLARIFICATION

Finding 6 — Distinction Between Reconsideration, Evolution, Downstream Departure, and Empirical Challenge
Exact tension:

A7's semantic distinctions table defines:

Reconsideration: re-evaluating whether current interpretation remains justified
Interpretation evolution: change from current to revised interpretation
Downstream departure: A6 Rule 12 reference
Empirical challenge: not explicitly defined as a distinct term
Within the binding rules, downstream departure (Rule 11) is treated as "not a revision trigger by itself" but "may become permissive if it reveals a substantive limitation."

Test:

Is "downstream departure becoming a permissive trigger when it reveals a substantive limitation" coherent?

A downstream departure is an explicit act by the researcher (A6 Rule 12). If the researcher explicitly departs and finds the departure reveals a problem, they can initiate reconsideration based on that finding. The trigger is actually the researcher's substantive finding, not the departure itself.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Minor. The formulation "downstream departure may become permissive trigger" is slightly circular — what actually triggers reconsideration is the substantive finding from the departure, not the departure per se. The current wording could lead to treating downstream departures themselves as triggers rather than what they reveal.

Minimum correction required:

Revise Rule 11 to clarify: downstream departure does not trigger reconsideration. If a downstream departure reveals a substantive incompatibility or limitation in the current interpretation, that finding may itself be a permissive trigger. The trigger is the finding, not the departure.

Classification: NARROW CLARIFICATION

Finding 7 — Revision Responsibility Assigned to Unresolved Procedure
Exact tension:

A7 repeatedly references "revision" being deferred: "A7 governs triggers for reconsideration/evolution, not the revision procedure itself." Section 8 lists "Procedure for performing an interpretation revision once reconsideration is triggered" as unresolved methodology content.

No ratified decision defines the revision procedure.

Test:

Is it coherent to ratify trigger criteria without a defined revision procedure?

Triggers can be defined independently of revision procedure in the same way that A5 identifies contradictions without defining how researchers reconcile them. The trigger is a precondition; the procedure follows it.

Upstream authority test:

A6 acknowledges that interpretations may evolve and A7 governs triggers. The revision procedure is acknowledged as unresolved. A6 Rule 12 and D1 Rules 3–4 together establish that evolution is permitted and provenance is preserved — sufficient to authorize trigger definition without fully defining the revision procedure.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

No. Defining triggers without defining revision procedure is coherent and explicitly scoped. The remaining-questions section correctly defers revision procedure.

Minimum correction required:

None.

Classification: NO CORRECTION REQUIRED

Finding 8 — Hidden Completeness Requirements or Review Obligations
Exact tension:

A7 Rule 2: "If a change materially affects the interpretation foundation... the researcher must reconsider the interpretation."

Does "must reconsider" create a hidden periodic review obligation or completeness requirement over unknown material?

Test:

A7 Rule 12 states: "Triggers are evaluated against the bounded available claim landscape (A6). The existence of unknown or undiscovered material is not itself a trigger."

This correctly bounds the completeness problem.

However: Rule 2's "must reconsider" applies whenever a material change occurs. If multiple material changes accumulate without reconsideration, does A7 impose a mandatory backlog review?

A7 does not address what happens when multiple mandatory triggers accumulate without reconsideration being performed. This could create an implied lifecycle obligation.

Upstream authority test:

D1 does not define a reconsideration schedule. A6 requires accounting for the available claim landscape at interpretation construction time but does not impose periodic review. No ratified decision authorizes a mandatory review cycle.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Minor latent ambiguity. "Must reconsider" could be interpreted as creating an immediate obligation on every trigger event, effectively creating a review lifecycle. A7 should clarify that mandatory reconsideration means the researcher must address the trigger before treating the current interpretation as a settled reference for new downstream decisions that depend on the changed foundation — not that every trigger requires an immediately performed review session.

Minimum correction required:

Clarify Rule 2: mandatory reconsideration means the researcher must account for the trigger before relying on the current interpretation for downstream decisions that depend on the changed foundation. No periodic review schedule or backlog obligation is implied.

# DECISION A7 — INTERPRETATION EVOLUTION TRIGGERS

**Status:** PROPOSED — awaiting semantic consistency audit  
**Upstream Authority:**
1. PROJECT MINORE — FOUNDING DEFINITION v0.1 (RATIFIED)
2. DECISION D1 — CONCEPT UNDERSTANDING: RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION LIFECYCLE (RATIFIED)
3. DECISION A1 — RESEARCH INPUT ADMISSIBILITY AND PROVENANCE (RATIFIED)
4. DECISION A2 — CLAIM EXTRACTION PROCEDURE (RATIFIED)
5. A2-C1 and A2-C2 (BINDING)
6. DECISION A3 — CLAIM CLASSIFICATION (RATIFIED)
7. A3-C1 (BINDING)
8. DECISION A4 — CONCEPT ASSOCIATION (RATIFIED)
9. DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE (RATIFIED)
10. DECISION A6 — RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION CONSTRUCTION (RATIFIED)

---

## 1. Exact Semantic Distinctions

| Term | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| **Current interpretation** | The current authoritative reference interpretation of a concept (D1; A6). |
| **Available research state** | The currently admitted and processed research inputs, extracted claims, preserved contexts, claim classifications, concept associations, and contradiction statuses (A1–A6). |
| **Reconsideration** | The act of re-evaluating whether the current interpretation remains justified given a change in the available research state. |
| **Interpretation evolution** | A change from the current interpretation to a revised interpretation. A7 governs when reconsideration/evolution is justified or required, not how revision is performed. |
| **Trigger** | A change in the available research state that justifies or requires reconsideration of the current interpretation. |
| **Mandatory trigger** | A trigger that requires the researcher to reconsider the interpretation (reconsideration is required, evolution is not guaranteed). |
| **Permissive trigger** | A trigger that allows reconsideration but does not require it. |
| **Insufficient trigger** | A change that does not justify reconsideration on its own. |
| **Materiality to interpretation foundation** | Whether a change impacts claims or reasoning that the current interpretation explicitly relied on as its foundation (A6). |
| **Downstream departure** | A downstream research step that explicitly departs from the current interpretation for a specific task without changing the interpretation itself (A6 Rule 12). |

---

## 2. Minimum Options

### Option A — Only new source claims trigger reconsideration
Reconsideration is justified only when a new claim is added.

**Problem:** Ignores other changes that alter meaning/structure of the same claim landscape (new context, reclassification, reassociation, contradiction re-status, empirical evidence). Too narrow to maintain provenance integrity.

### Option B — Any change triggers reconsideration
Any modification anywhere in the available research state requires reconsideration.

**Problem:** Overly sensitive; creates constant churn and procedural paralysis. Not aligned with “minimum decision” and does not distinguish material vs. immaterial changes.

### Option C — Material-change trigger model (foundation-sensitive)
Reconsideration is required or permitted based on whether a change materially affects:
- the interpretation foundation, or
- the contradiction/tension landscape relevant to the interpretation foundation, or
- empirical evidence that bears on claims the interpretation depends on.

This model distinguishes:
- mandatory triggers (foundation-impacting changes),
- permissive triggers (non-foundational but relevant changes),
- insufficient triggers (preference shifts without substantive reasoning).

**Verdict:** Option C is the minimum coherent model consistent with D1 (evolution + provenance) and A6 (explicit foundation + authoritative reference interpretation).

---

## 3. Trigger Determination Framework

A7 defines when reconsideration is **required**, **permitted**, or **not justified**, based on changes to the available research state.

### Step 1 — Identify the change type
Possible change types (not exhaustive ontology; procedural checklist):
- New source claim becomes available (A1–A2)
- Newly discovered preserved source context affecting an existing claim (A2/A3-C1)
- Change in claim classification or classification uncertainty (A3/A3-C1)
- Change in concept association (A4)
- Change in A5 status (Difference/Variation/Tension/Genuine Contradiction)
- New empirical evidence or results (later stages; may be recorded as research input/evidence)
- Researcher re-evaluation of unchanged landscape (A6 reasoning)
- Mere preference change without substantive reasoning
- Downstream research explicitly departing from the interpretation (A6 Rule 12)

### Step 2 — Determine materiality to interpretation foundation
Does the change affect claims, context, or contradiction statuses that the current interpretation explicitly relied on as its foundation?

- If **yes**: mandatory reconsideration trigger.
- If **no**, but the change concerns the same concept and is research-relevant: permissive reconsideration trigger.
- If **no**, and the change is unrelated or purely preference-based: insufficient trigger.

### Step 3 — Determine whether reconsideration is required, permitted, or not justified
- **Required** if the change:
  - introduces a new materially relevant claim that conflicts with or substantially refines the foundation, or
  - changes the meaning/applicability of a foundational claim via new preserved context, or
  - changes concept association/classification in a way that alters what claims are treated as foundational, or
  - changes A5 contradiction status involving foundational claims (e.g., tension → genuine contradiction), or
  - introduces empirical evidence that materially contradicts or undermines claims the interpretation relies on (without deciding evidence strength).

- **Permitted** if the change:
  - adds relevant claims that are supportive duplicates, peripheral refinements, or non-foundational extensions, or
  - adds empirical evidence that is weak/inconclusive but relevant, or
  - involves researcher re-reading/re-evaluating the same landscape with new reasoning (without new sources).

- **Not justified** if the change:
  - is a preference shift with no substantive re-evaluation or reasoning, or
  - attempts to silently substitute a different interpretation without explicit revision, or
  - consists only of downstream departure that is explicitly scoped and does not assert a change to the interpretation itself.

A7 does not decide whether reconsideration results in evolution; it decides when reconsideration is justified or required.

---

## 4. Binding Methodology Rules

1. **The current interpretation remains the authoritative reference interpretation until explicitly revised** (D1; A6). A7 defines when reconsideration is required or permitted; it does not itself revise the interpretation.

2. **Mandatory reconsideration triggers are foundation-sensitive.** If a change materially affects the interpretation foundation (claims, preserved context, or contradiction status the interpretation relied on), the researcher must reconsider the interpretation.

3. **New materially relevant conflicting claims require reconsideration, not automatic revision.** A new claim conflicting with the current interpretation triggers reconsideration, but the interpretation may remain unchanged after reconsideration with explicit reasoning.

4. **New preserved context affecting a foundational claim is a mandatory trigger.** If new context changes the meaning, scope, or applicability of a foundational claim, reconsideration is required.

5. **Changes in claim classification or classification uncertainty can be mandatory triggers** if they materially alter how a foundational claim should be understood (e.g., definitional vs. predictive role) or reveal that prior classification was uncertain in a way that affects the interpretation foundation.

6. **Changes in concept association can be mandatory triggers** if they add/remove substantively relevant claims from the concept’s available claim landscape in a way that materially affects the interpretation foundation.

7. **Changed A5 contradiction status is a mandatory trigger when it involves foundational claims.** If the contradiction landscape relevant to the foundation changes (e.g., tension → genuine contradiction), reconsideration is required.

8. **New empirical evidence can be a trigger without deciding evidential strength.** Empirical evidence that bears materially on claims the interpretation depends on triggers reconsideration; A7 does not decide whether evidence is strong enough to force evolution.

9. **Researcher re-evaluation without new research-state change is permitted, not mandatory.** The researcher may reconsider based on new reasoning applied to the unchanged available claim landscape, but such reconsideration must be grounded in explicit reasoning (A6) and is not forced.

10. **Preference change without substantive reasoning is insufficient.** A mere preference shift does not justify reconsideration or evolution.

11. **Downstream explicit departure does not itself change the interpretation.** Downstream research may explicitly depart from the current interpretation for a specific task (A6 Rule 12). This does not revise the current interpretation, but may serve as a permissive trigger for reconsideration if the departure reveals a substantive incompatibility or limitation.

12. **No completeness requirement beyond the available research state.** Triggers are evaluated against the bounded available research state (A6). The existence of unknown or undiscovered material is not itself a trigger.

---

## 5. Adversarial Tests

**Test 1 — New materially relevant claim directly conflicts with current interpretation (mandatory)**  
Current interpretation: “Order Blocks require bearish consolidation before bullish impulses.”  
New claim: “Order Blocks can be formed by any consolidation before any impulse direction.”  
- Change type: New claim available  
- Materiality: Impacts definition foundation  
- Trigger: **Mandatory reconsideration**  
- Outcome: Reconsideration required; evolution not automatic

**Test 2 — New claim duplicates existing support (permissive)**  
Current interpretation: “FVG is a three-candle imbalance (Claim A).”  
New claim: Another source repeats the same definition with similar scope.  
- Change type: New claim available  
- Materiality: Adds support but does not change foundation meaning  
- Trigger: **Permissive reconsideration** (update may be useful; not required)

**Test 3 — Newly discovered context changes meaning/applicability of a foundational claim (mandatory)**  
Foundational claim previously extracted: “FVGs always fill.”  
Newly recovered context: “...in non-trending conditions; in strong trends they may remain unfilled.”  
- Change type: Newly discovered preserved context affecting existing claim  
- Materiality: Directly modifies scope of foundational claim  
- Trigger: **Mandatory reconsideration**

**Test 4 — A5 reclassifies prior tension as genuine contradiction (mandatory when foundational)**  
Previously: two claims treated as Tension regarding OTE level.  
A5 update: after applying new preserved context, status becomes Genuine Contradiction for foundational claims.  
- Change type: A5 status change  
- Materiality: Contradiction landscape affecting foundation changed  
- Trigger: **Mandatory reconsideration**

**Test 5 — New empirical evidence contradicts the current interpretation (mandatory trigger for reconsideration)**  
Current interpretation includes predictive reliance: “FVGs tend to fill before continuation.”  
New empirical result: backtest shows no statistically meaningful fill tendency in the tested regime.  
- Change type: New empirical evidence  
- Materiality: Bears materially on claims interpretation relies on  
- Trigger: **Mandatory reconsideration** (A7 does not decide strength; only that reconsideration is required)

**Test 6 — Weak or inconclusive empirical results appear (permissive)**  
Empirical test produces mixed results with wide uncertainty.  
- Change type: New empirical evidence  
- Materiality: Relevant but inconclusive  
- Trigger: **Permissive reconsideration**

**Test 7 — Researcher changes mind after re-reading unchanged landscape (permissive)**  
No new claims/context/status changes; researcher develops a better synthesis of existing variations.  
- Change type: Researcher re-evaluation of unchanged landscape  
- Trigger: **Permissive reconsideration** (must be explicit reasoning; not mandatory)

**Test 8 — Preference change with no new reason (insufficient)**  
Researcher prefers a cleaner rule without re-evaluation or new information.  
- Change type: Preference shift only  
- Trigger: **Insufficient**

**Test 9 — Downstream research explicitly departs without changing interpretation (insufficient by itself; permissive if substantive)**  
A hypothesis test temporarily assumes an alternative definition for operationalization, explicitly noted as departure.  
- Change type: Downstream explicit departure  
- Trigger: **Not a revision trigger by itself**  
- May become **permissive** if the departure reveals a substantive limitation or incompatibility that warrants reconsideration

**Test 10 — New material affects a peripheral aspect only (permissive)**  
New claim concerns a niche session condition not referenced in the interpretation foundation.  
- Change type: New claim available  
- Materiality: Peripheral  
- Trigger: **Permissive reconsideration**

---

## 6. Recommended Decision

**Option C — Material-change trigger model (foundation-sensitive)**

A7 adopts a trigger framework where reconsideration of the current interpretation is:
- **mandatory** when the interpretation foundation (or its contradiction landscape) is materially affected,
- **permitted** when relevant changes occur without materially affecting the foundation,
- **not justified** when only preference shifts or silent substitutions are present.

A7 distinguishes reconsideration from actual interpretation evolution and does not define the revision procedure.

---

## 7. What A7 Unlocks

- Enables coherent, non-arbitrary evolution of interpretations consistent with D1 (evolution + historical provenance) and A6 (explicit foundation + authoritative reference interpretation).
- Enables downstream work to rely on a stable current interpretation while still supporting principled reconsideration when the research state materially changes.

---

## 8. Remaining Questions — Classified Only

| Question | Classification |
|----------|---------------|
| Procedure for performing an interpretation revision once reconsideration is triggered | Methodology content — outside A7 scope |
| How to document the reasoning for revision (format/structure) | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS / Methodology detail |
| How to represent trigger events and their linkage to interpretations | DEFER TO ARCHITECTURE |
| How to decide whether empirical evidence is strong enough to require evolution | DEFER TO M3 / later methodology |
| Whether reconsideration deadlines or periodic review are needed | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS |

---

## 9. Final Verdict

**DECISION A7 PROPOSED.**

A7 defines when reconsideration of the current interpretation is required, permitted, or not justified based on material changes to the available research state and to the interpretation foundation, while preserving the authoritative reference role of the current interpretation and deferring revision procedure to later methodology content.

---

## 10. Stop Statement

A7 proposed.

B1 has not begun.

Research Methodology has not been constructed.

Markdown

# DECISION A7 — INTERPRETATION EVOLUTION TRIGGERS

**Status:** PROPOSED — awaiting semantic consistency audit
**Upstream Authority:**
1. PROJECT MINORE — FOUNDING DEFINITION v0.1
2. DECISION D1 — CONCEPT UNDERSTANDING: RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION LIFECYCLE
3. DECISION A1 — RESEARCH INPUT ADMISSIBILITY AND PROVENANCE
4. DECISION A2 — CLAIM EXTRACTION PROCEDURE
5. A2-C1 and A2-C2 (BINDING)
6. DECISION A3 — CLAIM CLASSIFICATION
7. A3-C1 (BINDING)
8. DECISION A4 — CONCEPT ASSOCIATION
9. DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE
10. DECISION A6 — RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION CONSTRUCTION

---

## 1. Exact Semantic Distinctions

| Term | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| **Current interpretation** | The researcher's authoritative reference interpretation of a concept per A6, in force until explicitly revised. |
| **Research state** | The current bounded set of admitted, extracted, classified, associated claims and their A5 contradiction statuses relevant to a concept, per A6's available claim landscape. |
| **Reconsideration** | The act of examining whether the current interpretation should be re-evaluated in light of a change in the research state. Reconsideration does not itself change the interpretation. |
| **Interpretation change (evolution)** | The actual replacement of the current interpretation with a revised interpretation. The revision procedure is A6/downstream territory; A7 governs only when reconsideration is triggered. |
| **Trigger** | A change in the research state that creates a condition for reconsideration of the current interpretation. |
| **Mandatory trigger** | A change that requires the researcher to reconsider the current interpretation. Reconsideration is compulsory; interpretation change is not guaranteed. |
| **Permissive trigger** | A change that permits but does not require reconsideration. |
| **Insufficient trigger** | A change or event that does not by itself justify reconsideration. |
| **Foundational claim** | A claim that materially informs the current interpretation's foundation per A6 Step 3. |
| **Peripheral claim** | A claim associated with the concept that does not materially inform the current interpretation's foundation. |

**Critical distinction:** A trigger initiates reconsideration, not interpretation change. A mandatory trigger compels the researcher to examine whether the interpretation should change; it does not compel the interpretation to change. The outcome of reconsideration may be that the interpretation is retained unchanged.

---

## 2. Minimum Options

### Option A — Any Research-State Change Triggers Mandatory Reconsideration
Every change to the research state (new claim, new context, changed status) requires reconsideration.

**Problem:** Overwhelms the researcher with reconsideration obligations for immaterial changes (e.g., a duplicate claim, a peripheral addition). Violates the minimum-decision principle. Fails to distinguish material from immaterial changes.

### Option B — No Automatic Triggers; Reconsideration Always Discretionary
The researcher reconsiders only when they choose to; no change compels reconsideration.

**Problem:** Permits the researcher to ignore a directly conflicting new claim or a changed A5 contradiction status. Violates A6 Rule 2 (must account for materially relevant claims) and undermines the integrity of the interpretation as accountable to the claim landscape.

### Option C — Materiality-Based Triggers with Mandatory/Permissive/Insufficient Distinction
A change triggers reconsideration based on whether it materially affects the interpretation foundation. Changes are classified as mandatory triggers (compel reconsideration), permissive triggers (allow reconsideration), or insufficient (do not justify reconsideration).

**Test against A6:** Preserves the current interpretation as authoritative until revised; requires accountability to material claims; does not force interpretation change.

**Test against D1:** Permits evolution (Rule 3); preserves the single current interpretation (Rule 2); does not mandate change.

**Verdict:** Option C is the minimum viable and founding-consistent standard.

---

## 3. Trigger Determination Framework

A change in the research state is evaluated for its trigger status using the following framework.

**Step 1 — Identify the nature of the change**
Determine what has changed in the research state:
- A new source claim has become available
- Newly discovered context affects an existing claim
- Concept association has changed (A4)
- Claim classification or classification uncertainty has changed (A3)
- A5 contradiction status has changed
- New empirical evidence has appeared
- The researcher seeks to re-evaluate an unchanged claim landscape

**Step 2 — Determine materiality to the interpretation foundation**
Assess whether the change affects a foundational claim (materially informs the current interpretation per A6 Step 3) or only a peripheral aspect.

- If the change affects a foundational claim or the foundational reasoning: material
- If the change affects only peripheral claims or aspects not informing the foundation: non-material

**Step 3 — Classify the trigger**
Based on the nature and materiality of the change:

- **Mandatory trigger:** The change materially affects the interpretation foundation in a way that could alter the interpretation. The researcher must reconsider.
- **Permissive trigger:** The change is relevant but does not clearly compel reconsideration. The researcher may reconsider.
- **Insufficient trigger:** The change does not justify reconsideration by itself.

**Step 4 — Reconsideration outcome (boundary marker only)**
If reconsideration is triggered, the researcher examines whether the interpretation should change. The outcome may be:
- Interpretation retained unchanged (with reasoning)
- Interpretation change initiated (revision procedure is A6/downstream territory, not A7)

A7 governs only whether reconsideration is triggered. A7 does not govern the revision itself.

---

## 4. Binding Methodology Rules

1. **A trigger initiates reconsideration, not interpretation change.** Reconsideration is the examination of whether the interpretation should change. The interpretation may be retained unchanged following reconsideration.

2. **Triggers are classified as mandatory, permissive, or insufficient.** Mandatory triggers compel reconsideration; permissive triggers allow it; insufficient triggers do not justify it.

3. **A new source claim that materially conflicts with or materially affects the foundation of the current interpretation is a mandatory trigger.** Per A6 Rule 2, materially relevant claims cannot be silently dismissed; a materially conflicting new claim compels reconsideration.

4. **A new source claim that merely duplicates existing foundational support is an insufficient trigger.** Duplication adds no new material consideration to the interpretation foundation.

5. **Newly discovered context that changes the meaning or applicability of a foundational claim is a mandatory trigger.** Per A2 and A3-C1, context determines claim meaning; a change in foundational claim meaning materially affects the interpretation.

6. **A change in A5 contradiction status affecting a foundational claim is a mandatory trigger.** A7 inherits contradiction status from A5 and does not re-perform contradiction determination. If A5 reclassifies a foundational tension as a genuine contradiction (or vice versa), reconsideration is compelled.

7. **A change in concept association (A4) affecting whether a foundational claim belongs to the concept is a mandatory trigger.** If a foundational claim is disassociated, or a new materially relevant claim is associated, reconsideration is compelled. A7 inherits association status from A4.

8. **A change in claim classification or classification uncertainty (A3) affecting a foundational claim is a permissive trigger.** A change in the semantic role of a foundational claim may warrant reconsideration but does not automatically compel it, since the interpretation may still hold under the revised classification. A7 inherits classification status from A3.

9. **New empirical evidence relevant to the current interpretation is a mandatory trigger for reconsideration.** A7 determines only that empirical evidence creates a reconsideration condition; it does not assess the evidence's strength. Evidence-strength grading is M3 territory. Whether the evidence actually changes the interpretation is determined during reconsideration, not by A7.

10. **Weak or inconclusive empirical results are a permissive trigger.** Their existence permits reconsideration but does not compel it, since A7 does not grade evidence strength (M3 territory). The researcher may reconsider in light of any empirical result.

11. **Researcher re-evaluation of an unchanged claim landscape is a permissive trigger only if accompanied by substantive reasoning.** Per D1 Rule 3, interpretation may evolve; per A6, changes require explicit reasoning. Re-examination with new substantive reasoning permits reconsideration.

12. **A preference change without new material or substantive reasoning is an insufficient trigger.** Mere preference does not justify reconsideration, consistent with A6's prohibition on silent or unreasoned interpretation change.

13. **Downstream research questioning, testing, contradicting, or explicitly departing from the current interpretation is a permissive trigger.** Per A6 Rule 12, downstream research may depart from the interpretation with documented reasoning. Such departure permits the researcher to reconsider but does not itself compel reconsideration or change the interpretation.

14. **A change affecting only peripheral claims or aspects not informing the interpretation foundation is an insufficient trigger.** Non-foundational changes do not compel reconsideration, though the researcher may reconsider at discretion.

15. **A7 does not govern the revision procedure.** A7 determines only whether reconsideration is triggered. How an interpretation is revised, versioned, or preserved is governed by A6 and downstream decisions.

---

## 5. Adversarial Tests

**Test 1 — New materially relevant claim directly conflicting with current interpretation**

Current interpretation: "Order Blocks require bearish consolidation before bullish impulses."
New claim (Source 4): "Order Blocks can form from bullish consolidation before bullish impulses."

- Nature: New source claim
- Materiality: Directly conflicts with a foundational claim of the interpretation
- Trigger classification: **Mandatory trigger** (Rule 3)
- Reconsideration outcome: The researcher must reconsider. Outcome may be retention (if the new claim is judged less foundational) or change (revision territory).
- Framework handling: ✓ Materially conflicting claim compels reconsideration without forcing change

---

**Test 2 — New claim that duplicates existing support**

Current interpretation: "A Fair Value Gap is a three-candle imbalance."
New claim (Source 5): "Fair Value Gaps are three-candle imbalance structures." (duplicates existing foundational claim)

- Nature: New source claim
- Materiality: Duplicates existing foundational support; adds no new material consideration
- Trigger classification: **Insufficient trigger** (Rule 4)
- Reconsideration outcome: No reconsideration compelled
- Framework handling: ✓ Duplicate support does not trigger reconsideration

---

**Test 3 — Newly discovered context changing meaning of a foundational claim**

Current interpretation: Foundation includes Claim A: "Optimal Trade Entry occurs at 62% retracement."
Newly discovered context: The source passage for Claim A continues: "...but only in trending markets; in ranging markets, use 50%."

- Nature: Newly discovered context affecting an existing foundational claim
- Materiality: Changes the meaning/applicability of a foundational claim (adds conditional scope)
- Trigger classification: **Mandatory trigger** (Rule 5)
- Reconsideration outcome: The researcher must reconsider, as the foundational claim's meaning has materially changed
- Framework handling: ✓ Context change to foundational claim compels reconsideration

---

**Test 4 — A5 reclassifies a prior tension as a genuine contradiction**

Current interpretation: Bracketed a tension between two foundational claims about Liquidity Voids, provisionally adopting one position.
A5 status change: A5 reclassifies the tension as a genuine contradiction after applying newly available context.

- Nature: Change in A5 contradiction status affecting foundational claims
- Materiality: Foundational; the tension the interpretation relied upon is now a genuine contradiction
- Trigger classification: **Mandatory trigger** (Rule 6)
- Reconsideration outcome: The researcher must reconsider. A7 inherits the A5 status; does not re-perform contradiction determination.
- Framework handling: ✓ A5 status change to foundational claims compels reconsideration; A5 authority preserved

---

**Test 5 — New empirical evidence contradicts current interpretation**

Current interpretation: "Fair Value Gaps always fill."
New empirical evidence: A backtest shows 30% of Fair Value Gaps remained unfilled over the test period.

- Nature: New empirical evidence relevant to the interpretation
- Materiality: Directly relevant to a foundational assertion
- Trigger classification: **Mandatory trigger** (Rule 9)
- Reconsideration outcome: The researcher must reconsider. A7 determines only that the evidence creates a reconsideration condition; the strength/validity of the backtest is M3 territory, assessed during reconsideration.
- Framework handling: ✓ Empirical evidence compels reconsideration without A7 grading its strength

---

**Test 6 — Weak or inconclusive empirical results appear**

Current interpretation: "Order Blocks precede reversals in most cases."
New empirical evidence: A small, inconclusive test with a wide confidence interval and insufficient sample size.

- Nature: New empirical evidence (weak/inconclusive)
- Materiality: Relevant but inconclusive
- Trigger classification: **Permissive trigger** (Rule 10)
- Reconsideration outcome: The researcher may reconsider but is not compelled. A7 does not grade the evidence (M3 territory).
- Framework handling: ✓ Weak evidence permits but does not compel reconsideration; A7 does not assess strength

---

**Test 7 — Researcher changes mind after re-reading the same unchanged claim landscape**

Current interpretation: "Order Blocks require bearish consolidation."
Change basis: The researcher re-reads the same claims and forms new substantive reasoning: "Claims B and C provide broader scope than Claim A; integration is more justified than single-source dependence."

- Nature: Researcher re-evaluation of an unchanged claim landscape, with new substantive reasoning
- Materiality: Affects the foundational synthesis
- Trigger classification: **Permissive trigger** (Rule 11)
- Reconsideration outcome: The researcher may reconsider, justified by the new substantive reasoning
- Framework handling: ✓ Re-evaluation with substantive reasoning permits reconsideration

---

**Test 8 — Researcher changes preference with no new reason or substantive re-evaluation**

Current interpretation: "Optimal Trade Entry is at 62%."
Change basis: The researcher simply prefers 50% now, with no new material and no substantive reasoning.

- Nature: Preference change without new material or substantive reasoning
- Materiality: None (no substantive basis)
- Trigger classification: **Insufficient trigger** (Rule 12)
- Reconsideration outcome: No reconsideration justified
- Framework handling: ✓ Mere preference does not trigger reconsideration; consistent with A6's prohibition on unreasoned change

---

**Test 9 — Downstream research explicitly departs from current interpretation without changing it**

Current interpretation: "Market Maker stop-loss hunting is intentional."
Downstream event: A hypothesis test in B1/M2 explicitly departs from this interpretation, testing the alternative that it is unintentional, with documented reasoning.

- Nature: Downstream research departing from the interpretation (permitted per A6 Rule 12)
- Materiality: The departure signals a research position questioning the interpretation
- Trigger classification: **Permissive trigger** (Rule 13)
- Reconsideration outcome: The researcher may reconsider in light of the documented departure but is not compelled. The current interpretation remains authoritative until explicitly revised (A6 preserved).
- Framework handling: ✓ Downstream departure permits but does not compel reconsideration; A6 authority preserved

---

**Test 10 — New material affects a peripheral aspect but not the interpretation foundation**

Current interpretation of Fair Value Gap: Foundation is the three-candle structural definition and price-inefficiency mechanism.
New claim (Source 6): "Fair Value Gaps are more commonly discussed in intraday than swing trading." (peripheral; concerns discussion frequency, not the concept's definition or mechanism)

- Nature: New source claim affecting a peripheral aspect
- Materiality: Non-foundational; does not affect the interpretation foundation
- Trigger classification: **Insufficient trigger** (Rule 14)
- Reconsideration outcome: No reconsideration compelled, though the researcher may reconsider at discretion
- Framework handling: ✓ Peripheral change does not trigger mandatory reconsideration

---

## 6. Recommended Decision

**Option C — Materiality-Based Triggers with Mandatory/Permissive/Insufficient Distinction**

A change in the research state triggers reconsideration of the current interpretation based on its materiality to the interpretation foundation:

- **Mandatory triggers:** materially conflicting new claims; context changes to foundational claims; A5 status changes to foundational claims; concept-association changes to foundational claims; new empirical evidence relevant to the interpretation
- **Permissive triggers:** classification/classification-uncertainty changes to foundational claims; weak/inconclusive empirical results; researcher re-evaluation with substantive reasoning; downstream departure from the interpretation
- **Insufficient triggers:** duplicate claims; preference changes without substantive reasoning; peripheral changes not affecting the foundation

A7 governs only whether reconsideration is triggered. It does not govern the revision procedure, does not grade empirical evidence, does not re-perform A5/A4/A3 determinations, and does not compel interpretation change.

---

## 7. What A7 Unlocks

- The interpretation lifecycle is now complete for the A-series: interpretations are constructed (A6) and their reconsideration is governed (A7), while D1 preserves prior interpretations and the single current authoritative interpretation.
- **B1 — Research-Question Identification** may now proceed on a stable footing, because the conditions under which interpretations are reconsidered are defined, allowing research questions to reference stable current interpretations while acknowledging when they may evolve.
- Downstream empirical work (M2, M3) can now feed back into the interpretation lifecycle through the empirical-evidence trigger without A7 pre-judging evidence strength.

---

## 8. Remaining Questions — Classified Only

| Question | Classification |
|----------|---------------|
| The revision procedure once reconsideration is triggered | Methodology content — A6/downstream, not A7 |
| How reconsideration outcomes are recorded | DEFER TO ARCHITECTURE |
| Evidence-strength grading that informs reconsideration outcomes | M3 territory — not A7 |
| Whether reconsideration must occur within a time bound | Methodology content — not blocking B1 |
| How to detect that a research-state change has occurred | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS/ARCHITECTURE |
| Whether AI may flag potential triggers | DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS |
| Treatment of triggers for association-pending claims (no established concept) | Methodology content — not blocking B1 |
| Whether multiple simultaneous triggers change reconsideration priority | Methodology content — not blocking B1 |

---

## 9. Final Verdict

**DECISION A7 PROPOSED.**

Trigger model: Materiality-based classification of research-state changes into mandatory, permissive, and insufficient triggers for reconsideration of the current interpretation.

Core boundary: A7 governs when reconsideration is triggered, not how interpretation is revised. Reconsideration does not compel interpretation change; the interpretation may be retained unchanged.

Authority inheritance: A7 inherits contradiction status from A5, association status from A4, and classification status from A3 without re-performing those determinations. A7 responds to empirical evidence as a reconsideration condition without grading its strength (M3 territory).

Relationship to ratified upstream: Consistent with D1 (interpretation may evolve; single current interpretation preserved), A6 (current interpretation remains authoritative until explicitly revised; downstream may depart with reasoning; silent substitution prohibited), A5/A4/A3 (status inheritance), A2/A3-C1 (context determines claim meaning).

No contradiction with any ratified authority detected.

**B1 — Research-Question Identification is now unblocked.**

A7 proposed.

Task:

Determine the minimum methodology decision required to govern when an existing current researcher interpretation becomes eligible, required, or not justified for reconsideration or evolution.

A7 must answer the trigger question only:

What changes in the research state justify or require reconsideration of the current authoritative interpretation?

You must distinguish at minimum between:

a new source claim becoming available;
newly discovered source context affecting an existing claim;
a change in concept association;
a change in claim classification or classification uncertainty;
a change in A5 contradiction status;
new empirical evidence;

Adversarially determine whether these are:

sufficient triggers;
mandatory reconsideration triggers;
permissive triggers only;
insufficient by themselves.
Critical semantic boundaries:

Do not define how an interpretation is revised.
A7 governs triggers for reconsideration/evolution, not the revision procedure itself.

Do not define versioning, storage, historical-state mechanisms, or architecture.

Do not define empirical evidence-strength grading.
M3 owns Experimental Rigor and Evidence Assessment.

Do not decide whether new empirical evidence is strong enough to change an interpretation.
A7 may determine whether evidence creates a reconsideration condition, but not its evidential weight.

Do not re-perform A5 contradiction classification.
A7 may respond to a changed A5 status but must inherit that status from A5.

Do not reclassify claims under A3 or reassociate them under A4.
