# DECISION A6 — RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION CONSTRUCTION

**Status:** PROPOSED — awaiting semantic consistency audit
**Upstream Authority:**
1. PROJECT MINORE — FOUNDING DEFINITION v0.1
2. DECISION D1 — CONCEPT UNDERSTANDING: RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION LIFECYCLE
3. DECISION A1 — RESEARCH INPUT ADMISSIBILITY AND PROVENANCE
4. DECISION A2 — CLAIM EXTRACTION PROCEDURE
5. A2-C1 and A2-C2 (BINDING)
6. DECISION A3 — CLAIM CLASSIFICATION
7. A3-C1 (BINDING)
8. DECISION A4 — CONCEPT ASSOCIATION
9. DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE

---

## 1. Exact Semantic Distinctions

| Term | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| **Source claim** | An extracted, classified, and concept-associated claim attributed to a research input source (A1–A4). Immutable and attributable per D1. |
| **Claim landscape** | The complete set of source claims associated with a concept, including all differences, variations, tensions, and genuine contradictions identified per A5. |
| **Researcher interpretation** | The researcher's current authoritative understanding of a concept, constructed from the claim landscape. It represents what the researcher currently believes about the concept. |
| **Interpretation foundation** | The subset of source claims and reasoning from the claim landscape that directly inform the researcher's interpretation. |
| **Interpretation reasoning** | The explicit logical or evidential reasoning by which the researcher arrived at their interpretation from the claim landscape. |
| **Source-derived interpretation** | An interpretation that directly reflects or synthesizes what sources explicitly stated. |
| **Researcher synthesis** | An interpretation that synthesizes, integrates, or generalizes from multiple source claims in ways that go beyond any single source's explicit assertion. |
| **Provisional interpretation** | An interpretation that explicitly acknowledges uncertainty, unresolved contradiction, or pending empirical evidence. |
| **Authoritative interpretation** | The researcher's current, formally adopted position on what a concept means or encompasses. It is binding on downstream research decisions until the researcher explicitly revises it. |
| **Interpretation evolution** | A change in interpretation from one version to another. The prior version is preserved per D1 Rule 4. |

---

## 2. Minimum Options

### Option A — Source Consensus Required
The researcher may construct an interpretation only when all associated source claims agree or can be fully reconciled.

**Problem:** Directly contradicts D1 Rule 5 and A5 (which explicitly permit unresolved contradictions). Blocks interpretation construction whenever disagreement exists. Fails to answer: how does the researcher proceed with concept work when sources conflict?

### Option B — Researcher Selection without Constraint
The researcher may construct any interpretation without requirement to account for or acknowledge the claim landscape.

**Problem:** Violates D1's requirement to preserve reasoning chain (D1 Capability 13). Interpretation becomes arbitrary; provenance is lost. Researcher interpretation would be indistinguishable from source claims.

### Option C — Constrained Researcher Authority with Reasoning Requirement
The researcher constructs an interpretation from the claim landscape while required to:
- Account for all materially relevant claims (avoid silent dismissal of evidence)
- Explicitly reason from the claim landscape
- Preserve the full reasoning chain
- Acknowledge unresolved contradictions or tensions when present
- Permit provisional interpretations that acknowledge uncertainty

**Test against D1:** Preserves researcher authority (D1 Rule 2), permits evolution (D1 Rule 3), requires provenance chain (D1 Capability 13), permits unresolved contradictions (D1 Rule 5).

**Test against A5:** Respects contradiction determination; does not force false reconciliation.

**Verdict:** Option C is the minimum viable and founding-consistent standard.

---

## 3. Interpretation Construction Procedure

The following procedure governs how the researcher constructs an authoritative interpretation of a concept from the associated claim landscape.

**Step 1 — Gather the complete claim landscape**
Identify all extracted source claims associated with the concept per A4. Include all identified differences, variations, tensions, and genuine contradictions per A5.

**Step 2 — Assess the claim landscape**
Determine:
- Are all claims in broad agreement? (High consensus case)
- Are there compatible variations (different scope, context, emphasis)?
- Are there unresolved tensions (difficulty of reconciliation without full resolution)?
- Are there genuine unresolved contradictions?
- Are some claims more relevant to core concept definition than others?

**Step 3 — Determine the interpretation foundation**
Select which claims will directly inform the interpretation. This selection must be explicit and reasoned.

Selection may exclude claims based on:
- Relevance to core concept vs. peripheral variations
- Scope or applicability conditions
- Explicit judgment that a claim is less reliable (per A1 Rule 3, uncertainty does not disqualify; however, the researcher may weigh evidence of reliability)
- Competing claims that appear more foundational

Selection may **not** exclude claims based on:
- Silent dismissal without acknowledgment
- Mere disagreement with researcher preference
- Source authority alone (per A5 Rule 8)

**Step 4 — Construct the interpretation**
Formulate the researcher's authoritative understanding of the concept. The interpretation may:

a) **Reflect source consensus** — if all material claims agree, state the interpretation as consensus-derived

b) **Synthesize compatible variations** — if claims are variations on a compatible theme, synthesize into a unified understanding that encompasses the variations

c) **Acknowledge unresolved tension** — if claims create tension without strict incompatibility, construct an interpretation that acknowledges the tension explicitly

d) **Bracket genuine contradiction** — if genuine unresolved contradictions exist, construct an interpretation that provisionally adopts a position while explicitly preserving the contradiction

e) **Remain provisional** — acknowledge that the interpretation is provisional pending resolution of contradiction, additional source material, or empirical evidence

**Step 5 — Document the reasoning chain**
Preserve explicit connection between:
- The interpretation adopted
- The claim landscape considered
- Materiality judgments about which claims were foundational
- Treatment of contradictions and tensions
- Any explicit acknowledgment of uncertainty or provisionality

**Step 6 — Establish interpretation as authoritative**
The researcher formally adopts this interpretation as their current understanding of the concept. This interpretation is binding on downstream research work (B1, M2, etc.) unless explicitly revised.

---

## 4. Binding Methodology Rules

1. **Researcher interpretation is the researcher's authoritative current position on what a concept means or encompasses.** It is distinct from source claims (immutable, attributable) and from empirical validation (tested later). It represents the researcher's reasoned judgment.

2. **Interpretation must account for the claim landscape.** The researcher must consider all materially relevant source claims associated with the concept. Silent dismissal of claims is prohibited.

3. **Interpretation foundation must be explicit.** The researcher must state which claims form the foundation of the interpretation and why. If claims are excluded from the foundation, the exclusion must be reasoned.

4. **Researcher authority permits interpretation beyond source consensus.** The researcher may construct interpretations that:
   - Synthesize multiple source claims into unified understanding not explicitly stated by any single source
   - Adopt a position in response to tension or contradiction, making an authoritative choice
   - Acknowledge provisionality and unresolved uncertainty

5. **Unresolved contradictions must not be silently reconciled.** If A5 identifies a genuine unresolved contradiction, the interpretation may provisionally adopt a position, but the contradiction must be explicitly preserved in the interpretation reasoning chain.

6. **Interpretations may be provisional.** An interpretation may explicitly acknowledge:
   - Unresolved contradiction in the source landscape
   - Pending empirical validation
   - Uncertainty pending additional source material
   - Tension requiring future resolution

A provisional interpretation is still authoritative and binding on downstream work unless explicitly revised.

7. **Interpretation does not alter source claims.** Per D1, source claims remain immutable and attributable. Interpretation is constructed from claims, not by rewriting them.

8. **Interpretation reasoning must preserve the full chain per D1 Capability 13.** The reasoning connecting interpretation to claim landscape must be explicit and traceable.

9. **Interpretation is not empirical validation.** The researcher's interpretation of what a concept means is distinct from evidence that the concept is true or effective in markets. Later empirical testing (M2, M3) may support or contradict the interpretation without invalidating the interpretation itself.

10. **Source authority does not override reasoning.** Per A5 Rule 8, a claim from a highly reputable source does not automatically become the interpretation foundation. The researcher's reasoning must justify the choice.

11. **Interpretation evolution is preserved.** When interpretation changes, the prior version is preserved as historical provenance per D1 Rule 4. The reasoning for the change is documented.

---

## 5. Interpretation Construction Procedure — Detailed Application

**Step 1 input:** The complete claim landscape for the concept

**Step 2 assessment:** Classify the landscape into categories (consensus, variations, tensions, contradictions)

**Step 3 foundation selection:**
- Identify core defining claims
- Identify peripheral or conditional claims
- Mark claims excluded from foundation (with reasoning)
- Mark tensions and contradictions that will be addressed in Step 4

**Step 4 construction:** Formulate interpretation using one or more patterns:
- Consensus: "All sources agree that [concept] means [X]"
- Synthesized variation: "Sources describe [concept] with different emphases on [variations]. The interpretation integrates these as [synthesis]"
- Acknowledged tension: "Sources present [concept] with some interpretive difficulty between [positions]. The interpretation provisionally adopts [position] while noting the tension"
- Bracketed contradiction: "Sources conflict on whether [concept] requires [property]. The interpretation provisionally adopts [position] and preserves the unresolved contradiction"
- Provisional: "The interpretation of [concept] is [X], provisionally pending [specific uncertainty/evidence]"

**Step 5 output:** Documented reasoning chain connecting interpretation to landscape

**Step 6 authorization:** Formal adoption as current authoritative understanding

---

## 6. Adversarial Tests

**Test 1 — Broad agreement across sources**

Concept: Fair Value Gap
Source Claims:
- Claim A (Source 1): "A Fair Value Gap is a three-candle imbalance where candles 1 and 3 wicks do not overlap"
- Claim B (Source 2): "Fair Value Gaps are price inefficiencies created when price moves without trading in a zone"
- Claim C (Source 3): "FVGs form when price gaps over support/resistance levels without filling the gap"

Claim Landscape Assessment:
- Claim A: Structural definition
- Claim B: Mechanism description
- Claim C: Outcome/behavior description
- All three are compatible; they describe different aspects of the same phenomenon

Interpretation Construction:
- Foundation: All three claims inform the core definition
- Synthesis: "A Fair Value Gap is a three-candle imbalance structure (Claim A) that represents price inefficiency (Claim B) and typically results in gap-fill behavior (Claim C). These aspects are complementary and mutually reinforcing."
- Provisional status: Not provisional; consensus across sources
- Framework handling: ✓ Synthesizes multiple compatible variations into unified interpretation

---

**Test 2 — Multiple compatible variations**

Concept: Order Block
Source Claims:
- Claim A (Source 1): "Order Blocks form from bearish consolidation before bullish impulses"
- Claim B (Source 2): "Order Blocks form from the last consolidation before any impulse, regardless of direction"
- Claim C (Source 3): "Order Blocks are consolidations that precede significant price movements; the specific impulse direction varies"

Claim Landscape Assessment:
- Claim A: Specific (bearish consolidation → bullish impulse)
- Claim B: General (consolidation → any impulse)
- Claim C: General with variations
- Tension: Claim A is more specific; Claims B and C are broader. Not contradictory, but varying scope

Interpretation Construction:
- Foundation: All three claims; Claim A is more specific, Claims B and C capture breadth
- Synthesis: "An Order Block is a consolidation that precedes an impulse. While impulses can be directional (Claim A's bearish-before-bullish pattern) or bidirectional (Claims B, C), the core definition is the consolidation-impulse relationship. Sources suggest the bearish-to-bullish pattern is common but not universal."
- Provisional status: Not provisional; the variation is resolvable as scope differences
- Framework handling: ✓ Interpretation encompasses variations by distinguishing core definition from scope variations

---

**Test 3 — Unresolved tension**

Concept: Optimal Trade Entry
Source Claims:
- Claim A (Source 1): "Optimal Trade Entry occurs at the 61.8% retracement of the impulse"
- Claim B (Source 2): "Optimal Trade Entry occurs at the 50% retracement"
- Claim C (Source 3): "Optimal Trade Entry varies based on impulse strength; typically 50–70% retracement"

Claim Landscape Assessment:
- Claim A and B: Conflicting specific numbers (61.8% vs. 50%)
- Claim C: Range acknowledgment
- Tension: Multiple sources assert different specific entry levels; Claim C acknowledges variation but does not fully resolve the conflict

Contradiction Analysis (A5): Is this a genuine contradiction?
- Claim A asserts 61.8% specifically
- Claim B asserts 50% specifically
- If applied to the same impulse under the same conditions, both cannot be optimal
- Genuine contradiction (unresolved per A5)

Interpretation Construction:
- Foundation: All three claims; acknowledge that sources conflict on the specific level
- Interpretation (provisional): "Optimal Trade Entry in this framework is provisionally interpreted as occurring in the 50–70% retracement range, with debate in source material between 50% (Claim B) and 61.8% (Claim A) as the ideal point. The tension between these specific levels remains unresolved."
- Provisional status: Yes — explicitly acknowledges the unresolved contradiction
- Framework handling: ✓ Interpretation acknowledges unresolved contradiction without forcing false reconciliation; remains authoritative despite provisionality

---

**Test 4 — Genuine unresolved contradiction**

Concept: Market Maker Intent
Source Claims:
- Claim A (Source 1): "Market Makers actively hunt stop-losses to accumulate liquidity for their trades"
- Claim B (Source 2): "Market Makers' stop-loss hunting is a side effect of their natural order flow, not intentional"

Claim Landscape Assessment:
- Direct logical incompatibility: intentional vs. unintentional behavior
- Applied to the same market conditions and same Market Makers
- No context resolves this contradiction (A5 Step 2)
- Genuine unresolved contradiction per A5

Interpretation Construction:
- Foundation: Both claims present; researcher acknowledges the contradiction
- Interpretation (bracketed contradiction): "The interpretation of Market Maker Intent is that Market Makers' behavior creates stop-loss hunting effects (both sources agree on the behavior). However, sources disagree on whether this is intentional (Claim A) or a side effect of natural order flow (Claim B). The interpretation provisionally treats it as intentional behavior while explicitly preserving the contradiction about intent."
- Provisional status: Yes — explicitly brackets and preserves the unresolved contradiction
- Researcher choice: The researcher has selected intentionality as the provisional foundation, but justified the choice by acknowledging the competing claim
- Framework handling: ✓ Interpretation does not force reconciliation; does not silently dismiss Claim B; makes an authoritative choice while preserving the contradiction

---

**Test 5 — Researcher selecting one claim over another without empirical evidence**

Concept: Liquidity Void
Source Claims:
- Claim A (Source 1, highly reputable): "Liquidity Voids are zones without market participants; price will eventually fill them"
- Claim B (Source 2, lower provenance confidence): "Liquidity Voids are temporary phenomena that often close without price filling the zone"

Claim Landscape Assessment:
- Competing claims about the same phenomenon
- No unresolved contradiction per A5 (both could be true under different conditions if qualified)
- Tension: Source A asserts voids will fill; Source B asserts they often close without filling
- Source A has higher authority/reputation

Interpretation Construction (NOT RECOMMENDED):
- If reasoning is: "Source A is more reputable, therefore adopt Claim A as interpretation"
- Framework handling: ✗ Violates Rule 10 (source authority does not override reasoning)

Interpretation Construction (CORRECT):
- Foundation: Acknowledge both claims; reason from landscape
- Interpretation: "Liquidity Voids are zones without market participants. The interpretation acknowledges tension in source material about their resolution: Source A asserts they eventually fill; Source B asserts they often close without filling. The distinction may depend on timeframe or market conditions not yet fully specified in source material. The interpretation provisionally adopts [specific position] based on [reasoning about which conditions dominate in the research domain], while acknowledging the competing perspective."
- Provisional status: Yes — acknowledges tension and dependence on unspecified conditions
- Framework handling: ✓ Researcher makes a choice but justifies it by reasoning about conditions, not by source authority alone; preserves competing claim; makes reasoned assertion

---

**Test 6 — Researcher constructing interpretation beyond what any single source states**

Concept: Institutional Trading Pattern Recognition
Source Claims:
- Claim A (Source 1): "Institutions create Order Blocks through consolidation"
- Claim B (Source 2): "Institutions create Fair Value Gaps through rapid price movement"
- Claim C (Source 3): "Institutions use both consolidation and rapid movement to manage liquidity"

Claim Landscape Assessment:
- All three claims are compatible; each describes part of institutional behavior
- No single source states a complete integrated framework
- Researcher synthesis is possible and appropriate

Interpretation Construction:
- Foundation: All three claims; researcher integrates them
- Synthesis (researcher-derived): "Institutional Trading Pattern Recognition in this framework encompasses two primary mechanisms: Order Blocks (consolidation-based liquidity management, Claim A) and Fair Value Gaps (movement-based liquidity management, Claim B). These represent complementary tactics within an integrated liquidity-management strategy (Claim C). The interpretation synthesizes these into a two-mechanism framework."
- Provisional status: Not provisional; synthesis is well-supported by the claim landscape
- Researcher contribution: Integration of three distinct claims into a unified framework
- Framework handling: ✓ Researcher goes beyond single-source assertion but remains grounded in claim landscape; reasoning is traceable and authoritative

---

**Test 7 — New relevant source material appearing after interpretation**

Researcher has established interpretation (from Test 6):
"Institutional Trading Pattern Recognition encompasses Order Blocks and Fair Value Gaps as complementary liquidity-management mechanisms."

New Source Material:
- Claim D (Source 4, discovered after interpretation): "Institutions also use psychological price levels (round numbers) as a third liquidity-management mechanism, distinct from Order Blocks and FVGs"

Interpretation Evolution Trigger:
- New material is materially relevant (addresses institutional patterns)
- New material is domain-relevant per A2-C1
- New material cannot be ignored without violating Rule 2 (account for claim landscape)

Interpretation Revision (A7 territory, but A6 must permit revision):
- Prior interpretation preserved as version 1
- Reasoning for revision documented: "New source material identifies a third institutional mechanism (psychological levels) not captured in prior interpretation"
- New interpretation (version 2): "Institutional Trading Pattern Recognition encompasses three primary mechanisms: Order Blocks, Fair Value Gaps, and psychological price levels. These represent complementary tactics..."
- Prior version is retained as historical provenance per D1 Rule 4

Framework handling: ✓ A6 permits interpretation evolution without reopening ratified decisions; prior version is preserved

---

**Test 8 — Researcher changing interpretation without new source material**

Researcher's Prior Interpretation:
"Order Blocks require bearish consolidation before bullish impulses"

Researcher's Revised Interpretation (same sources):
"Order Blocks form from any consolidation before any impulse direction; the bearish-before-bullish pattern is frequent but not required"

Change Basis:
- Not new source material
- Re-examination of existing source landscape
- Researcher has shifted synthesis of existing claims (Test 2 situation)
- No new evidence, no new sources

Interpretation Evolution Trigger:
- This is a researcher judgment shift, not forced by new material
- Permitted per D1 Rule 3 (interpretation may evolve)
- Requires explicit reasoning for the change

Reasoning Requirement:
- Prior interpretation was based on: "Claim A (source 1) was treated as definitive"
- New interpretation is based on: "Claim A is more specific; Claims B, C provide broader scope. Integration of all three is more accurate than accepting Claim A alone"
- Reasoning documents the change: shift from treating one claim as foundational to synthesizing multiple compatible claims

Documentation:
- Prior version preserved
- Reasoning for change explicit: "Re-examination of claim landscape reveals that sources B and C provide scope beyond source A; integration is more justified than single-source dependence"

Framework handling: ✓ A6 permits researcher judgment evolution with documented reasoning; prior version is retained; change is traceable

---

## 7. What A6 Unlocks

- **A7 — Interpretation Evolution Triggers** may now proceed, because the methodology establishes how interpretations are constructed and that they may evolve; A7 will determine the conditions and procedures for formal interpretation revision.
- **B1 — Research-Question Identification** may now proceed, because the methodology can identify unanswered research questions within the established interpretation landscape.
- **B2 (testability criteria), M2 (hypothesis formation), and downstream research work** can now reference the researcher's authoritative interpretation of each concept as a foundation.

---

## 9. Final Verdict

**DECISION A6 PROPOSED.**

Interpretation construction: Constrained researcher authority model with explicit reasoning requirement and claim-landscape accountability.

Interpretation foundation: Selected from complete claim landscape per explicit reasoning; unresolved contradictions and tensions preserved; provisional interpretations permitted.

Researcher authority: Permits synthesis beyond single-source claims and provisional positioning on contradictions; does not permit silent dismissal of material claims or authority-based override of reasoning.

Relationship to ratified upstream: Consistent with D1 (researcher authority, interpretation evolution, reasoning chain), A1 (claim admissibility), A2 (claim extraction fidelity), A4 (concept association), A5 (contradiction preservation).

No contradiction with any ratified authority detected.

**A7 — Interpretation Evolution Triggers is now unblocked.**

DECISION A6 — RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION CONSTRUCTION
Status: PROPOSED — awaiting semantic consistency audit
Upstream Authority:

PROJECT MINORE — FOUNDING DEFINITION v0.1
DECISION D1 — CONCEPT UNDERSTANDING: RESEARCHER INTERPRETATION LIFECYCLE
DECISION A1 — RESEARCH INPUT ADMISSIBILITY AND PROVENANCE
DECISION A2 — CLAIM EXTRACTION PROCEDURE
A2-C1 and A2-C2 (BINDING)
DECISION A3 — CLAIM CLASSIFICATION
A3-C1 (BINDING)
DECISION A4 — CONCEPT ASSOCIATION
DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE
1. Exact Semantic Distinctions
Term	Meaning
Source claim	An extracted, classified, and concept-associated claim attributed to a research input source (A1–A4). Immutable and attributable per D1.
Claim landscape	The complete set of source claims associated with a concept, including all differences, variations, tensions, and genuine contradictions identified per A5.
Researcher interpretation	The researcher's current authoritative understanding of a concept, constructed from the claim landscape. It represents what the researcher currently believes about the concept.
Interpretation foundation	The subset of source claims and reasoning from the claim landscape that directly inform the researcher's interpretation.
Interpretation reasoning	The explicit logical or evidential reasoning by which the researcher arrived at their interpretation from the claim landscape.
Source-derived interpretation	An interpretation that directly reflects or synthesizes what sources explicitly stated.
Researcher synthesis	An interpretation that synthesizes, integrates, or generalizes from multiple source claims in ways that go beyond any single source's explicit assertion.
Provisional interpretation	An interpretation that explicitly acknowledges uncertainty, unresolved contradiction, or pending empirical evidence.
Authoritative interpretation	The researcher's current, formally adopted position on what a concept means or encompasses. It is binding on downstream research decisions until the researcher explicitly revises it.
Interpretation evolution	A change in interpretation from one version to another. The prior version is preserved per D1 Rule 4.
2. Minimum Options
Option A — Source Consensus Required
The researcher may construct an interpretation only when all associated source claims agree or can be fully reconciled.

Problem: Directly contradicts D1 Rule 5 and A5 (which explicitly permit unresolved contradictions). Blocks interpretation construction whenever disagreement exists. Fails to answer: how does the researcher proceed with concept work when sources conflict?

Option B — Researcher Selection without Constraint
The researcher may construct any interpretation without requirement to account for or acknowledge the claim landscape.

Problem: Violates D1's requirement to preserve reasoning chain (D1 Capability 13). Interpretation becomes arbitrary; provenance is lost. Researcher interpretation would be indistinguishable from source claims.

Option C — Constrained Researcher Authority with Reasoning Requirement
The researcher constructs an interpretation from the claim landscape while required to:

Account for all materially relevant claims (avoid silent dismissal of evidence)
Explicitly reason from the claim landscape
Preserve the full reasoning chain
Acknowledge unresolved contradictions or tensions when present
Permit provisional interpretations that acknowledge uncertainty
Test against D1: Preserves researcher authority (D1 Rule 2), permits evolution (D1 Rule 3), requires provenance chain (D1 Capability 13), permits unresolved contradictions (D1 Rule 5).

Test against A5: Respects contradiction determination; does not force false reconciliation.

Verdict: Option C is the minimum viable and founding-consistent standard.

3. Interpretation Construction Procedure
The following procedure governs how the researcher constructs an authoritative interpretation of a concept from the associated claim landscape.

Step 1 — Gather the complete claim landscape
Identify all extracted source claims associated with the concept per A4. Include all identified differences, variations, tensions, and genuine contradictions per A5.

Step 2 — Assess the claim landscape
Determine:

Are all claims in broad agreement? (High consensus case)
Are there compatible variations (different scope, context, emphasis)?
Are there unresolved tensions (difficulty of reconciliation without full resolution)?
Are there genuine unresolved contradictions?
Are some claims more relevant to core concept definition than others?
Step 3 — Determine the interpretation foundation
Select which claims will directly inform the interpretation. This selection must be explicit and reasoned.

Selection may exclude claims based on:

Relevance to core concept vs. peripheral variations
Scope or applicability conditions
Explicit judgment that a claim is less reliable (per A1 Rule 3, uncertainty does not disqualify; however, the researcher may weigh evidence of reliability)
Competing claims that appear more foundational
Selection may not exclude claims based on:

Silent dismissal without acknowledgment
Mere disagreement with researcher preference
Source authority alone (per A5 Rule 8)
Step 4 — Construct the interpretation
Formulate the researcher's authoritative understanding of the concept. The interpretation may:

a) Reflect source consensus — if all material claims agree, state the interpretation as consensus-derived

b) Synthesize compatible variations — if claims are variations on a compatible theme, synthesize into a unified understanding that encompasses the variations

c) Acknowledge unresolved tension — if claims create tension without strict incompatibility, construct an interpretation that acknowledges the tension explicitly

d) Bracket genuine contradiction — if genuine unresolved contradictions exist, construct an interpretation that provisionally adopts a position while explicitly preserving the contradiction

e) Remain provisional — acknowledge that the interpretation is provisional pending resolution of contradiction, additional source material, or empirical evidence

Step 5 — Document the reasoning chain
Preserve explicit connection between:

The interpretation adopted
The claim landscape considered
Materiality judgments about which claims were foundational
Treatment of contradictions and tensions
Any explicit acknowledgment of uncertainty or provisionality
Step 6 — Establish interpretation as authoritative
The researcher formally adopts this interpretation as their current understanding of the concept. This interpretation is binding on downstream research work (B1, M2, etc.) unless explicitly revised.

4. Binding Methodology Rules
Researcher interpretation is the researcher's authoritative current position on what a concept means or encompasses. It is distinct from source claims (immutable, attributable) and from empirical validation (tested later). It represents the researcher's reasoned judgment.

Interpretation must account for the claim landscape. The researcher must consider all materially relevant source claims associated with the concept. Silent dismissal of claims is prohibited.

Interpretation foundation must be explicit. The researcher must state which claims form the foundation of the interpretation and why. If claims are excluded from the foundation, the exclusion must be reasoned.

Researcher authority permits interpretation beyond source consensus. The researcher may construct interpretations that:

Synthesize multiple source claims into unified understanding not explicitly stated by any single source
Adopt a position in response to tension or contradiction, making an authoritative choice
Acknowledge provisionality and unresolved uncertainty
Unresolved contradictions must not be silently reconciled. If A5 identifies a genuine unresolved contradiction, the interpretation may provisionally adopt a position, but the contradiction must be explicitly preserved in the interpretation reasoning chain.

Interpretations may be provisional. An interpretation may explicitly acknowledge:

Unresolved contradiction in the source landscape
Pending empirical validation
Uncertainty pending additional source material
Tension requiring future resolution
A provisional interpretation is still authoritative and binding on downstream work unless explicitly revised.

Interpretation does not alter source claims. Per D1, source claims remain immutable and attributable. Interpretation is constructed from claims, not by rewriting them.

Interpretation reasoning must preserve the full chain per D1 Capability 13. The reasoning connecting interpretation to claim landscape must be explicit and traceable.

Interpretation is not empirical validation. The researcher's interpretation of what a concept means is distinct from evidence that the concept is true or effective in markets. Later empirical testing (M2, M3) may support or contradict the interpretation without invalidating the interpretation itself.

Source authority does not override reasoning. Per A5 Rule 8, a claim from a highly reputable source does not automatically become the interpretation foundation. The researcher's reasoning must justify the choice.

Interpretation evolution is preserved. When interpretation changes, the prior version is preserved as historical provenance per D1 Rule 4. The reasoning for the change is documented.

5. Interpretation Construction Procedure — Detailed Application
Step 1 input: The complete claim landscape for the concept

Step 2 assessment: Classify the landscape into categories (consensus, variations, tensions, contradictions)

Step 3 foundation selection:

Identify core defining claims
Identify peripheral or conditional claims
Mark claims excluded from foundation (with reasoning)
Mark tensions and contradictions that will be addressed in Step 4
Step 4 construction: Formulate interpretation using one or more patterns:

Consensus: "All sources agree that [concept] means [X]"
Synthesized variation: "Sources describe [concept] with different emphases on [variations]. The interpretation integrates these as [synthesis]"
Acknowledged tension: "Sources present [concept] with some interpretive difficulty between [positions]. The interpretation provisionally adopts [position] while noting the tension"
Bracketed contradiction: "Sources conflict on whether [concept] requires [property]. The interpretation provisionally adopts [position] and preserves the unresolved contradiction"
Provisional: "The interpretation of [concept] is [X], provisionally pending [specific uncertainty/evidence]"
Step 5 output: Documented reasoning chain connecting interpretation to landscape

Step 6 authorization: Formal adoption as current authoritative understanding

6. Adversarial Tests
Test 1 — Broad agreement across sources

Concept: Fair Value Gap
Source Claims:

Claim A (Source 1): "A Fair Value Gap is a three-candle imbalance where candles 1 and 3 wicks do not overlap"
Claim B (Source 2): "Fair Value Gaps are price inefficiencies created when price moves without trading in a zone"
Claim C (Source 3): "FVGs form when price gaps over support/resistance levels without filling the gap"
Claim Landscape Assessment:

Claim A: Structural definition
Claim B: Mechanism description
Claim C: Outcome/behavior description
All three are compatible; they describe different aspects of the same phenomenon
Interpretation Construction:

Foundation: All three claims inform the core definition
Synthesis: "A Fair Value Gap is a three-candle imbalance structure (Claim A) that represents price inefficiency (Claim B) and typically results in gap-fill behavior (Claim C). These aspects are complementary and mutually reinforcing."
Provisional status: Not provisional; consensus across sources
Framework handling: ✓ Synthesizes multiple compatible variations into unified interpretation
Test 2 — Multiple compatible variations

Concept: Order Block
Source Claims:

Claim A (Source 1): "Order Blocks form from bearish consolidation before bullish impulses"
Claim B (Source 2): "Order Blocks form from the last consolidation before any impulse, regardless of direction"
Claim C (Source 3): "Order Blocks are consolidations that precede significant price movements; the specific impulse direction varies"
Claim Landscape Assessment:

Claim A: Specific (bearish consolidation → bullish impulse)
Claim B: General (consolidation → any impulse)
Claim C: General with variations
Tension: Claim A is more specific; Claims B and C are broader. Not contradictory, but varying scope
Interpretation Construction:

Foundation: All three claims; Claim A is more specific, Claims B and C capture breadth
Synthesis: "An Order Block is a consolidation that precedes an impulse. While impulses can be directional (Claim A's bearish-before-bullish pattern) or bidirectional (Claims B, C), the core definition is the consolidation-impulse relationship. Sources suggest the bearish-to-bullish pattern is common but not universal."
Provisional status: Not provisional; the variation is resolvable as scope differences
Framework handling:** ✓ Interpretation encompasses variations by distinguishing core definition from scope variations
Test 3 — Unresolved tension

Concept: Optimal Trade Entry
Source Claims:

Claim A (Source 1): "Optimal Trade Entry occurs at the 61.8% retracement of the impulse"
Claim B (Source 2): "Optimal Trade Entry occurs at the 50% retracement"
Claim C (Source 3): "Optimal Trade Entry varies based on impulse strength; typically 50–70% retracement"
Claim Landscape Assessment:

Claim A and B: Conflicting specific numbers (61.8% vs. 50%)
Claim C: Range acknowledgment
Tension: Multiple sources assert different specific entry levels; Claim C acknowledges variation but does not fully resolve the conflict
Contradiction Analysis (A5): Is this a genuine contradiction?

Claim A asserts 61.8% specifically
Claim B asserts 50% specifically
If applied to the same impulse under the same conditions, both cannot be optimal
Genuine contradiction (unresolved per A5)
Interpretation Construction:

Foundation: All three claims; acknowledge that sources conflict on the specific level
Interpretation (provisional): "Optimal Trade Entry in this framework is provisionally interpreted as occurring in the 50–70% retracement range, with debate in source material between 50% (Claim B) and 61.8% (Claim A) as the ideal point. The tension between these specific levels remains unresolved."
Provisional status: Yes — explicitly acknowledges the unresolved contradiction
Framework handling: ✓ Interpretation acknowledges unresolved contradiction without forcing false reconciliation; remains authoritative despite provisionality
Test 4 — Genuine unresolved contradiction

Concept: Market Maker Intent
Source Claims:

Claim A (Source 1): "Market Makers actively hunt stop-losses to accumulate liquidity for their trades"
Claim B (Source 2): "Market Makers' stop-loss hunting is a side effect of their natural order flow, not intentional"
Claim Landscape Assessment:

Direct logical incompatibility: intentional vs. unintentional behavior
Applied to the same market conditions and same Market Makers
No context resolves this contradiction (A5 Step 2)
Genuine unresolved contradiction per A5
Interpretation Construction:

Foundation: Both claims present; researcher acknowledges the contradiction
Interpretation (bracketed contradiction): "The interpretation of Market Maker Intent is that Market Makers' behavior creates stop-loss hunting effects (both sources agree on the behavior). However, sources disagree on whether this is intentional (Claim A) or a side effect of natural order flow (Claim B). The interpretation provisionally treats it as intentional behavior while explicitly preserving the contradiction about intent."
Provisional status: Yes — explicitly brackets and preserves the unresolved contradiction
Researcher choice: The researcher has selected intentionality as the provisional foundation, but justified the choice by acknowledging the competing claim
Framework handling: ✓ Interpretation does not force reconciliation; does not silently dismiss Claim B; makes an authoritative choice while preserving the contradiction
Test 5 — Researcher selecting one claim over another without empirical evidence

Concept: Liquidity Void
Source Claims:

Claim A (Source 1, highly reputable): "Liquidity Voids are zones without market participants; price will eventually fill them"
Claim B (Source 2, lower provenance confidence): "Liquidity Voids are temporary phenomena that often close without price filling the zone"
Claim Landscape Assessment:

Competing claims about the same phenomenon
No unresolved contradiction per A5 (both could be true under different conditions if qualified)
Tension: Source A asserts voids will fill; Source B asserts they often close without filling
Source A has higher authority/reputation
Interpretation Construction (NOT RECOMMENDED):

If reasoning is: "Source A is more reputable, therefore adopt Claim A as interpretation"
Framework handling: ✗ Violates Rule 10 (source authority does not override reasoning)
Interpretation Construction (CORRECT):

Foundation: Acknowledge both claims; reason from landscape
Interpretation: "Liquidity Voids are zones without market participants. The interpretation acknowledges tension in source material about their resolution: Source A asserts they eventually fill; Source B asserts they often close without filling. The distinction may depend on timeframe or market conditions not yet fully specified in source material. The interpretation provisionally adopts [specific position] based on [reasoning about which conditions dominate in the research domain], while acknowledging the competing perspective."
Provisional status: Yes — acknowledges tension and dependence on unspecified conditions
Framework handling: ✓ Researcher makes a choice but justifies it by reasoning about conditions, not by source authority alone; preserves competing claim; makes reasoned assertion
Test 6 — Researcher constructing interpretation beyond what any single source states

Concept: Institutional Trading Pattern Recognition
Source Claims:

Claim A (Source 1): "Institutions create Order Blocks through consolidation"
Claim B (Source 2): "Institutions create Fair Value Gaps through rapid price movement"
Claim C (Source 3): "Institutions use both consolidation and rapid movement to manage liquidity"

All three claims are compatible; each describes part of institutional behavior

Researcher synthesis is possible and appropriate

Synthesis (researcher-derived): "Institutional Trading Pattern Recognition in this framework encompasses two primary mechanisms: Order Blocks (consolidation-based liquidity management, Claim A) and Fair Value Gaps (movement-based liquidity management, Claim B). These represent complementary tactics within an integrated liquidity-management strategy (Claim C). The interpretation synthesizes these into a two-mechanism framework."
Provisional status: Not provisional; synthesis is well-supported by the claim landscape
Researcher contribution: Integration of three distinct claims into a unified framework
Framework handling: ✓ Researcher goes beyond single-source assertion but remains grounded in claim landscape; reasoning is traceable and authoritative
Test 7 — New relevant source material appearing after interpretation

Researcher has established interpretation (from Test 6):

New Source Material:

Interpretation Evolution Trigger:

New material is domain-relevant per A2-C1

Prior interpretation preserved as version 1

New interpretation (version 2): "Institutional Trading Pattern Recognition encompasses three primary mechanisms: Order Blocks, Fair Value Gaps, and psychological price levels. These represent complementary tactics..."
Prior version is retained as historical provenance per D1 Rule 4

Test 8 — Researcher changing interpretation without new source material

"Order Blocks require bearish consolidation before bullish impulses"

DECISION A1 — RESEARCH INPUT ADMISSIBILITY AND PROVENANCE
DECISION A2 — CLAIM EXTRACTION PROCEDURE
A2-C1 and A2-C2 (BINDING)
DECISION A3 — CLAIM CLASSIFICATION
A3-C1 (BINDING)
DECISION A4 — CONCEPT ASSOCIATION
1. Exact Semantic Distinctions
Term	Meaning
Difference	Two claims address the same concept but describe distinct aspects without conflicting. Both can be true simultaneously.
Variation	Two claims address the same concept with differing scope, emphasis, or specificity, but are not logically incompatible. May reflect context-dependence rather than conflict.
Tension	Two claims address the same concept in ways that create interpretive difficulty without strict logical incompatibility. Both may be defensible, but they are not easily reconciled.
Apparent contradiction	Two claims appear to conflict based on surface wording. This is a provisional diagnostic status applied before relevant preserved context is applied.
Genuine contradiction	Two or more claims, after applying all relevant preserved source context, cannot both be true simultaneously under the same or sufficiently similar conditions. They make logically incompatible assertions.
Unresolved contradiction	A genuine contradiction that has been identified and preserved but has not been reconciled by the researcher. It remains open.
Researcher reconciliation	The researcher's explicit interpretive act of resolving or explaining a contradiction. This is researcher interpretation (A6 territory), not part of A5.
2. Binding Methodology Rules
Contradiction determination concerns logical compatibility between assertions. Two claims are contradictory if they cannot both be true simultaneously under the same or sufficiently similar conditions, after all relevant preserved context has been applied.

Contradiction determination must apply all relevant preserved context. Per A2 and A3-C1, claims carry preserved source context. That context must be applied before contradiction status is assigned. Contradiction determination does not require exhaustive review of unrelated context or the source's entire body of work.

The claims must make substantively overlapping assertions such that logical compatibility can meaningfully be tested. Shared concept association per A4 is a strong indicator of such overlap, but identical association sets are not required. If claims make fundamentally distinct assertions about unrelated concerns, they are not contradictory.

All claims involved in a contradiction remain immutable and attributable. Per D1, source claims are not altered, merged, or suppressed upon identification of contradiction.

Contradictions are preserved as unresolved until the researcher explicitly reconciles them. Preservation without reconciliation is the default A5 state. Per D1 Rule 5, unresolved contradictions may persist indefinitely.

Forced reconciliation is prohibited. The research process may continue with an unresolved contradiction preserved.

Contradiction handling is distinct from classification, concept association, and researcher interpretation.

Classification (A3): what kind of assertion the source made — unchanged by contradiction status
Concept association (A4): what the claim is about — unchanged by contradiction status
Researcher interpretation (A6): the researcher's authoritative synthesis — the appropriate place for reconciliation
Contradiction is not validated or invalidated by source authority. A contradiction between a primary source and a secondary transcription is handled the same as a contradiction between two primary sources. Provenance confidence difference is a research-relevant fact but does not resolve contradiction.

After relevant preserved context is applied, an initially apparent conflict may resolve as Difference, Variation, or Tension, or may persist as a Genuine Contradiction. These are the four final classification outcomes. "Apparent Contradiction" is a provisional diagnostic status that is superseded by one of these four classifications once context is applied.

3. Contradiction Determination Procedure
The following procedure governs whether two or more claims about the same concept are determined to be contradictory.

Step 1 — Confirm substantively overlapping assertions
Both claims must make assertions with substantive overlap such that logical compatibility can meaningfully be tested. Shared concept association per A4 is strong evidence of overlap, but identical association sets are not required.

Step 2 — Apply all relevant preserved source context
Use all relevant preserved source context per A2 and A3-C1 for each claim.

Do the claims operate under different conditions (e.g., different sessions, instruments, timeframes)?
Do the claims come from different definitional contexts (e.g., one defines the concept narrowly; the other broadly)?
Does one claim use a term the other uses differently?
Does context for either claim establish domain restrictions, conditional qualifications, or explicit exceptions?
If relevant context restricts the domain of applicability or establishes genuine exceptions such that the apparent conflict dissolves: the claims are not a genuine contradiction. They are classified as Difference, Variation, or Tension depending on what the context reveals.

Step 3 — Test logical incompatibility
After all relevant context is applied, determine whether the claims can simultaneously be true under the same or sufficiently similar conditions.

If yes: not a genuine contradiction. Classify as Difference, Variation, or Tension and preserve both claims.
If no: proceed to Step 4.
Step 4 — Declare genuine contradiction
The claims are determined to be a genuine contradiction. Both claims remain immutable and attributable per D1. Neither is removed, merged, or suppressed.

Step 5 — Preserve as unresolved
The contradiction is preserved as unresolved. No reconciliation is required at A5. Reconciliation is researcher interpretation (A6 territory).

4. Adversarial Tests
Test 1 — Difference, not contradiction

Claim A (Source 1): "Fair Value Gaps require a three-candle structure"
Claim B (Source 2): "Fair Value Gaps represent price inefficiency that must be corrected"

Step 1: Both make assertions about Fair Value Gaps; overlap is clear ✓
Step 2: Context: Claim A concerns structural identification; Claim B concerns market behavior. No qualification or context that creates incompatibility.
Step 3: Both can simultaneously be true — structure definition and behavioral prediction are different aspects
Verdict: Difference. Not a contradiction.
Framework handling: ✓ Claims preserved without contradiction status
Test 2 — Universal quantifier with domain restriction

Claim A (Source 1): "Fair Value Gaps always fill"
Claim B (Source 2): "Fair Value Gaps in strong trend conditions often remain unfilled"

Step 1: Both make assertions about Fair Value Gap filling behavior; overlap is clear ✓
Step 2: Apply relevant context: Does preserved context for Claim A establish domain restrictions or conditions?
If Claim A context states: "always fill except in strong trending conditions" — this explicitly establishes an exception, and context resolves the conflict
If Claim A context states: "always fill before the next major move" — this is a timing qualification, not an exception; does not restrict domain to exclude strong trends
If Claim A context is silent — the universal "always" applies across all conditions
Step 3: If Claim A's "always" applies universally across all conditions (including strong trends) after context review, then Claim A (universal) and Claim B (asserts exceptions in strong trends) are logically incompatible
Step 4: Genuine contradiction declared
Step 5: Preserved as unresolved
Framework handling: ✓ Do not assume unstated exceptions; require explicit domain restriction or exception in context to resolve the contradiction
Test 3 — Different concept associations with overlapping assertions

Claim A (Source 1): "Every bullish reversal requires a preceding Order Block"
Associated with: Order Blocks, Bullish Reversals.

Claim B (Source 2): "Bullish reversals can occur without Order Blocks during volatile market conditions"
Associated with: Bullish Reversals, Volatility.

Step 1: Concept-association sets are not identical; however, both claims make substantively overlapping assertions about whether Order Blocks are necessary for bullish reversals ✓
Step 2: Apply relevant context: Does preserved context for Claim A exempt volatile conditions? If context is silent on volatility exceptions, Claim A's necessity claim applies across conditions.
Step 3: Claim A asserts necessity universally; Claim B asserts counterexamples under volatile conditions. If Claim A's context does not exclude volatile conditions, the claims are logically incompatible.
Step 4: Genuine contradiction declared despite different concept-association sets
Step 5: Preserved as unresolved
Framework handling: ✓ Substantive overlapping assertions about necessity matter; identical concept associations are not required
Test 4 — Tension after context application

Claim A (Source 1): "Order Blocks are the primary driver of reversals"
Claim B (Source 2): "Order Blocks are one of several factors creating reversal conditions"

Step 1: Both address Order Block role in reversals; overlap is clear ✓
Step 2: Relevant context: Does either claim use "primary" or "one of several" differently under different conditions? If context is silent, both interpretations remain possible.
Step 3: Claim A (primary driver) and Claim B (one of several factors) create interpretive tension. They are not strictly logically incompatible — one primary factor coexists with several other factors. But they suggest different causal models.
Verdict: Tension. Not a genuine contradiction, but not fully reconciled either.
Framework handling: ✓ Tension is a valid post-context outcome where conflict is reduced but not eliminated
Test 5 — Same source self-contradiction

Claim A (Source 1, 2019): "Institutional candles are identified by their body size relative to previous candles"
Claim B (Source 1, 2023): "Institutional candles are identified by their displacement away from consolidation, regardless of relative body size"

Step 1: Both identify properties of Institutional Candles; overlap is clear ✓
Step 2: Relevant context: Does the four-year gap indicate source evolution? If so, this is research-relevant but does not resolve contradiction. Both definitions remain incompatible if applied to the same candle simultaneously.
Step 3: Body-size test and displacement test yield different identification outcomes under the same conditions — logically incompatible
Step 4: Genuine contradiction declared, even within the same source
Step 5: Preserved as unresolved. Source self-contradiction is a research-relevant fact, not a resolution.
Framework handling: ✓ Source evolution is tracked; self-contradiction does not invalidate either claim
Test 6 — Authority asymmetry does not resolve contradiction

Claim A (Primary ICT video): "Optimal Trade Entry requires a 62% retracement level"
Claim B (Third-party transcription): "Optimal Trade Entry requires a 50% retracement level"

Step 1: Both address Optimal Trade Entry level; overlap is clear ✓
Step 2: Relevant context: Claim B comes from a third-party transcription (lower provenance confidence per A1). However, per A1 Rule 3, uncertain provenance does not disqualify a claim. Per Rule 8, authority does not resolve contradiction.
Step 3: 62% and 50% retracement cannot both define OTE simultaneously under the same conditions — logically incompatible
Step 4: Genuine contradiction declared
Step 5: Preserved as unresolved. Provenance confidence difference is additional research-relevant context, but does not automatically resolve the contradiction.
Framework handling: ✓ Authority asymmetry does not short-circuit contradiction determination
5. What A5 Unlocks
A6 — Researcher Interpretation Construction may now proceed with a complete picture of the source claim landscape for each concept, including identified contradictions. The researcher's interpretive task (A6) can explicitly address, explain, or provisionally resolve contradictions under a principled framework.
Concept understanding construction (D1 Rules 2–5) can now distinguish between agreed-upon source content and genuinely contested terrain.
6. Remaining Questions — Classified Only
Question	Classification
Mechanism for recording contradiction status and its metadata	Methodology content — not blocking A6
Whether a contradiction between extraction error and source claim is handled differently	Methodology content — not blocking A6
Whether AI may assist in identifying potential contradictions	DEFER TO REQUIREMENTS
How contradictions are stored, linked, or flagged	DEFER TO ARCHITECTURE
Whether contradiction status itself can be revised	Methodology content — not blocking A6
Quantitative evidence resolving a source contradiction	DEFER TO EVIDENCE GRADING (M3)
Whether researcher reconciliation in A6 permanently closes a contradiction	A6 territory — not A5
7. Ratification Record
Decision: A5 — Contradiction Handling Procedure
Status: RATIFIED
Ratification basis: Semantic Consistency Audit — five findings accepted and corrected per binding disposition; two final consistency corrections applied to adversarial tests
Corrections applied:
Replaced undefined "context exhaustion" language with sufficiency standard: "all relevant preserved context" required, not exhaustive or categorical exclusion of context beyond preserved record
Corrected Test 2 to properly recognize that temporal qualifications alone do not create exceptions to universal claims; domain restrictions or explicit exceptions are required to resolve contradiction
Corrected Test 3 to feature logically valid overlapping assertions: necessity of Order Blocks for bullish reversals tested across different concept-association sets
Corrected shared-concept requirement to permit substantive overlapping assertions even when concept-association sets are not identical
Corrected Rule 9 to permit Tension as a valid post-context outcome alongside Difference, Variation, and Genuine Contradiction
Clarified that "Apparent Contradiction" is a provisional diagnostic status superseded by one of four final classifications once relevant context is applied
No contradiction with Founding Definition v0.1, D1, A1, A2, A3, or A4 detected

A5 FINAL CONSISTENCY CORRECTION

The ratified A5 text is accepted in substance, but two adversarial tests contain logical errors that must be corrected before the ratified decision is treated as final.

Do not reopen A5.

Apply only the two corrections below.

TEST 2 — UNIVERSAL QUANTIFIER ERROR
The current test states:

“If Claim A context states: ‘always fill before the next major move’ — this may permit exceptions in strong trends (context resolves conflict).”

This is logically incorrect.

Adding a temporal condition (“before the next major move”) does not create an exception to the universal claim that Fair Value Gaps always fill. It changes when the fill is asserted to occur, not whether every Fair Value Gap fills.

Correct the test so that:

Claim A remains universal unless its preserved context actually restricts the domain of applicability or explicitly establishes an exception.
A contextual qualification resolves the contradiction only if it changes the scope of the universal assertion in a way that excludes the conditions described by Claim B.
Do not treat a timing qualification alone as an exception.
Use a logically valid example if needed, such as:

“If preserved context establishes that Claim A applies only to non-trending conditions, while Claim B applies to strong-trend conditions, the conflict may dissolve because the domains of applicability differ.”

If Claim A remains universal across the domain, and Claim B asserts genuine counterexamples within that domain, the claims are logically incompatible.

TEST 3 — NON-EQUIVALENT EVENTS
The current test compares:

Claim A: “Order Blocks must precede a bullish impulse.”
Claim B: “Reversals occur without Order Blocks during volatile market conditions.”

These claims are not necessarily contradictory.

A bullish impulse and a reversal are not established as the same event. Therefore, the claims do not yet make sufficiently overlapping assertions for logical incompatibility to be tested.

This test currently violates A5 Rules 1 and 3.

Replace Test 3 with a logically valid case involving different concept-association sets but genuinely overlapping assertions.

Use:

Claim A:
“Every bullish reversal requires a preceding Order Block.”
Associated with: Order Blocks, Bullish Reversals.

Claim B:
“Bullish reversals can occur without Order Blocks during volatile market conditions.”
Associated with: Bullish Reversals, Volatility.

Analysis:

The full concept-association sets are not identical.
The assertions substantively overlap on whether Order Blocks are necessary for bullish reversals.
If Claim A’s preserved context does not exclude volatile conditions, Claim A asserts necessity while Claim B asserts counterexamples.
The claims are logically incompatible under overlapping conditions.
Genuine Contradiction.
TASK

Produce the complete corrected final version of:

DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE
Requirements:

Keep Status: RATIFIED
Apply only these two test corrections and directly necessary consistency edits
Do not alter the binding methodology rules
Do not reopen any upstream decision
Do not add new methodology content
Do not begin A6
Preserve the existing Ratification Record, adding these two final consistency corrections if necessary
Output only the complete corrected A5
Stop after A5

A5 AUDIT DISPOSITION

The Semantic Consistency Audit is accepted in substance.

All five findings are accepted, subject to the corrected binding disposition below.

CONTEXT STANDARD — ACCEPTED WITH CORRECTED FORMULATION
The audit correctly rejects an undefined “context exhaustion” standard.

However, do not adopt the audit’s sentence:

“Context beyond the preserved record is not required.”

That formulation is too absolute and may improperly exclude newly discovered relevant context.

Use this binding correction instead:

“Contradiction determination must apply all available preserved context that is relevant to assessing the apparent incompatibility. It does not require exhaustive review of unrelated context or the source’s entire body of work.”

Do not introduce an absolute rule that context outside the currently preserved record can never become relevant.

TEST 2 LOGICAL VALIDITY — ACCEPTED
The audit is correct.

“Fair Value Gaps always fill” is a universal assertion.

“Fair Value Gaps in strong trend conditions often remain unfilled” asserts exceptions.

Unless preserved context for the first claim independently limits or qualifies its universal scope, the claims are logically incompatible.

Do not infer an unstated exception merely to dissolve the contradiction.

Correct Test 2 accordingly.

SHARED-CONCEPT REQUIREMENT — ACCEPTED WITH CORRECTED FORMULATION
The audit correctly identifies that contradiction analysis must not depend on identical concept-association sets.

However, do not use the proposed phrase:

“substantive relational involvement”

because it is insufficiently defined and may introduce unnecessary semantic structure.

Use this binding correction instead:

“The claims must make substantively overlapping assertions such that logical compatibility between them can meaningfully be tested. Shared concept association is a strong indicator of such overlap, but identical association sets are not required.”

Contradiction determination concerns compatibility between assertions, not identity of concept-association sets.

TENSION AS A VALID OUTCOME — ACCEPTED WITH CORRECTED RATIONALE
The audit correctly finds that Rule 9 improperly excludes Tension as a possible post-context outcome.

However, do not state or imply that:

“Tension is a form of unresolved-but-lessened contradiction.”

Under A5’s own definitions, Tension is not a contradiction because strict logical incompatibility has not been established.

Use this binding correction:

“After relevant preserved context is applied, an initially apparent conflict may resolve as Difference, Variation, or Tension, or may persist as Genuine Contradiction.”

APPARENT CONTRADICTION AS PROVISIONAL — ACCEPTED
“Apparent Contradiction” is a provisional diagnostic status, not a final post-analysis outcome.

Once relevant preserved context is applied, the claims must be more precisely determined as:

Difference
Variation
Tension
Genuine Contradiction
Do not retain “Apparent Contradiction” as a parallel final state after contradiction determination is complete.

TASK

Now produce the corrected final version:

DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE
Apply only:

the five accepted audit findings;
the corrected formulations in this disposition;
necessary consistency edits caused directly by those corrections.
REQUIREMENTS

Status: RATIFIED
Preserve A5’s core decision
Do not reopen D1, A1, A2, A3, or A4
Do not introduce architecture, schema, entities, tables, enums, confidence scores, or implementation mechanisms
Do not begin A6
Do not add new methodology decisions
Do not broaden A5 beyond contradiction determination and preservation
Remove all “context exhaustion” language
Do not state that context outside the preserved record is categorically irrelevant
Do not use “substantive relational involvement”
Do not describe Tension as a type or degree of contradiction
Ensure all adversarial tests are logically consistent with the corrected binding rules
Ensure the What A5 Unlocks section reflects only valid dependency relationships
Include a Ratification Record and Stop Statement
Before finalizing, perform an internal consistency check against the corrected rules.

Output only the complete corrected ratified Decision A5.

Stop after A5.

DECISION A5 — CONTRADICTION HANDLING PROCEDURE (PROPOSED, under audit)
Issue 1 Finding — Context Exhaustion Standard
Exact tension:

A5 requires:

"all available preserved source context" (Section 3, Step 2)
"full application of available context" (Rule 3)
"after context exhaustion" (Section 2, Option B)
These formulations create an absolute threshold: contradiction is only declared after exhausting all possible context.

However, "exhaustion" is potentially infinite or impractical. Available context for a claim may include:

immediate surrounding sentences
paragraph-level context
section-level context
the entire source document
the entire source series or body of work
external corroborating sources
historical knowledge of the source's evolution
Without a stopping rule, "exhaustion" becomes undefined.

Upstream dependency:

A2 Rule 2: "Extraction preserves what the source said, not what the researcher believes the source meant."
A3-C1: "Classification must use available preserved context."

Neither upstream rule requires absolute exhaustion. A2 specifies "preserved context carried with the claim" — a bounded set. A3-C1 specifies "preserved context [that] is available" — not all possible context, but what was actually preserved.

Necessity test:

Does A5 require absolute exhaustion to protect against false contradictions?

No. The protective rule is: "Context sufficient to determine whether incompatibility is condition-dependent must be applied before contradiction is declared."

This is a sufficiency standard, not an exhaustion standard.

Adversarial example:

Claim A: "Order Blocks require a minimum three-candle consolidation"
Claim B: "Order Blocks can form in a two-candle structure"

Preserved context for Claim A: none regarding whether "three-candle" is a hard minimum or a typical/preferred condition.
Preserved context for Claim B: explicit statement in source about "any consolidation structure that satisfies these conditions"

Must the researcher examine:

every sentence in both sources?
every related concept's description?
the entire author's body of work?
academic literature on consolidation patterns?
No. Context sufficient to determine whether the "three-candle" requirement is stated as absolute or conditional should be applied. If that preserved context indicates "three-candle is typical, not required," then the contradiction may dissolve. If preserved context remains silent, the contradiction stands as unresolved.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes. A5 imposes an undefined "exhaustion" standard that is either impractical (infinite) or vague (how much is "enough"?).

Minimum correction required:

Replace "exhaustion" language with sufficiency language:

"Contradiction determination uses preserved source context sufficient to assess whether apparent incompatibility is condition-dependent or reflects genuinely incompatible assertions. All preserved context carried with each claim (per A2) must be applied. Context beyond the preserved record is not required."

Classification: CORRECTION REQUIRED

Issue 2 Finding — Test 2 Logical Validity
Exact tension:

Test 2 presents:

Claim A: "Fair Value Gaps always fill"
Claim B: "Fair Value Gaps in strong trend conditions often remain unfilled"

A5 concludes: "With context applied, Claim B's condition may explain when Claim A's 'always' breaks — not logically incompatible if Claim B is understood as a contextual qualifier."

Logical analysis:

"Always" is a universal quantifier: ∀ FVG, FVG fills.

"In strong trends, FVG often remain unfilled" means: ∃ conditions where FVG do not fill.

These are directly contradictory unless Claim A's universality excludes strong-trend conditions.

Test 2's resolution assumes: "Claim B's condition may explain when Claim A breaks" — this assumes Claim A did not already exclude strong trends.

Necessity test:

Is the assumption valid?

Only if preserved context for Claim A explicitly states or implies that "always" is qualified by conditions (e.g., "always fills, except in strong trends"). If preserved context for Claim A is silent on strong trends, then Claim A's "always" remains universal.

Adversarial example:

Claim A context: "Fair Value Gaps always fill before the next major move."
Claim B: "In strong trend conditions, Fair Value Gaps often remain unfilled."

With Claim A's context applied: "always fills before the next major move" — this could be compatible with B if "strong trend conditions" contain ongoing major moves that prevent the fill.

But without Claim A stating "before the next major move," the universal "always" contradicts B.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes. Test 2 assumes an unstated exception in Claim A without establishing it from preserved context. The conclusion ("not logically incompatible") is not necessarily true.

Minimum correction required:

Revise Test 2:

"With full preserved context applied: Claim A states 'always fill' — is this qualified by conditions in its preserved context? If not, Claim A asserts a universal. Claim B asserts exceptions exist. These are logically incompatible unless Claim A's context independently establishes that 'always' excludes strong-trend conditions."

Classify outcome as: Possible genuine contradiction or possible Tension (depending on whether context for Claim A contains unstated qualifications). Do not assume compatibility.

Classification: CORRECTION REQUIRED

Issue 3 Finding — Shared-Concept Requirement
Exact tension:

A5 Step 1: "Both claims must be associated with the same concept per A4."

But A4 permits:

multiple concept associations per claim;
relational claims involving multiple concepts;
claims where association sets overlap but are not identical.
Test case:

Claim A: associated with Fair Value Gaps, Price Reversal (Relational claim about both)
Claim B: associated with Fair Value Gaps, Market Structure (Relational claim about both)

Both claims are associated with Fair Value Gaps. But Claim A also involves Price Reversal; Claim B also involves Market Structure.

A5 Step 1's "same concept" requirement would permit contradiction analysis between A and B (both share Fair Value Gaps). But the substantive incompatibility might actually concern Price Reversal vs. Market Structure, not Fair Value Gaps.

Upstream dependency:

A4 explicitly permits multi-concept association for relational claims. A5 should operationalize this.

Necessity test:

Is "exact same concept association" necessary, or is "shared substantive concern" sufficient?

Sufficient. Two claims can be contradictory even if their concept associations are not identical, provided their substantive assertions overlap in a way that creates logical incompatibility.

Example:
Claim A: "Order Blocks precede reversals in all conditions" (associated with Order Blocks, Reversals)
Claim B: "Reversals occur without Order Blocks during volatile conditions" (associated with Reversals, Volatility, Order Blocks)

These claims share substantive concern (the necessity of Order Blocks for reversals) even though their full association sets differ.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes. A5 Step 1 may be too restrictive. Requiring exact shared concept association may miss contradictions whose incompatibility involves relational or overlapping associations.

Minimum correction required:

Revise A5 Step 1:

"Both claims must involve the same concept substantively, either by direct association or by substantive relational involvement. If one claim is relational (involving multiple concepts), check whether its subject matter overlaps with the other claim's subject matter."

Do not force a strict "same concept" check that ignores relational complexity.

Classification: CORRECTION REQUIRED

Issue 4 Finding — Apparent-Contradiction Outcome and Tension Classification
Exact tension:

A5 Semantic Distinctions defines: Tension ("Two claims... create interpretive difficulty without being strictly logically incompatible").

A5 Rule 9 states: "Apparent contradictions dissolved by context are preserved as Differences or Variations."

This rule lists only Differences and Variations as post-context outcomes, omitting Tension.

Yet the Semantic Distinctions table exists and describes Tension as a distinct state.

Logical analysis:

The definitions allow four outcomes for an apparent contradiction after context application:

Difference — distinct aspects, not incompatible
Variation — different scope/emphasis, not incompatible
Tension — interpretive difficulty without strict incompatibility
Genuine Contradiction — persists after context exhaustion
A5 should permit any of these as outcomes. Rule 9 restricts to Difference or Variation, excluding Tension.

Tension is a valid post-context state: context reduces the conflict but does not fully resolve it. Example: "always" vs. "often in strong trends" — context partially resolves by narrowing the domain of "always," but ambiguity remains about whether "always" is truly qualified or merely probabilistically challenged.

Upstream dependency:

D1 permits unresolved contradictions. Tension is a form of unresolved-but-lessened contradiction. It is consistent with D1.

Necessity test:

Should Tension be a permitted post-context outcome?

Yes. It reflects a common research scenario: context reduces but does not eliminate conflict.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Yes. Rule 9 unnecessarily excludes Tension as a valid outcome.

Minimum correction required:

Revise Rule 9:

"Apparent contradictions addressed by context may be resolved into Difference or Variation (fully compatible once context is applied). Alternatively, they may remain as Tension (context reduces but does not eliminate interpretive difficulty) or as genuine contradictions (context fails to resolve logical incompatibility). The classification reflects the outcome of full context application."

Preserve Tension as a permitted classification outcome.

Classification: CORRECTION REQUIRED

Additional Issues Review
Issue 5 — "Apparent Contradiction" as persistent state

A5 Semantic Distinctions defines "Apparent contradiction" as a provisional diagnosis (implied by "apparent"). But it is not clear whether once context is applied, the "apparent" label is removed or retained.

If a claim initially appears contradictory but context dissolves it into a Difference, does "apparent contradiction" remain a permanent label, or is it superseded by "Difference"?

Necessity test:

A5 should clarify that "Apparent Contradiction" is a provisional state that is replaced by a more precise classification (Difference, Variation, Tension, or genuine Contradiction) once context is applied.

Whether a real inconsistency exists:

Ambiguity exists. Not a hard inconsistency, but a clarity issue.

Minimum correction required:

Clarify in Semantic Distinctions:

"Apparent contradiction: A provisional diagnosis applied to two claims that initially appear to conflict. This status is replaced by a more precise classification (Difference, Variation, Tension, or genuine Contradiction) once preserved context is applied."

Classification: NARROW CLARIFICATION

Minimum Corrections Required

Issue 1: Context Exhaustion	Replace "exhaustion" language with sufficiency: "Contradiction determination uses preserved source context sufficient to assess whether apparent incompatibility is condition-dependent. All preserved context carried with each claim must be applied. Context beyond the preserved record is not required."
Issue 2: Test 2 Logical Validity	Revise Test 2 to not assume unstated exceptions in Claim A. Require preserved context for Claim A to establish whether "always" is truly qualified by conditions. If context is silent, recognize the contradiction as unresolved.
Issue 3: Shared-Concept Requirement	Revise A5 Step 1 to permit substantive relational involvement: "Both claims must involve the same concept substantively, either by direct association or by substantive relational involvement."
Issue 4: Tension as Valid Outcome	Revise Rule 9 to permit Tension as a post-context outcome: "Apparent contradictions addressed by context may be resolved into Difference or Variation, may remain as Tension, or may persist as genuine contradictions."
